The formal arbitration path (§14.11) uses BCAEN evidence as documentary record precisely because the protocol incidentally produces evidence quality that no dedicated documentation service can replicate.

## 7. Extended Capabilities

*The same infrastructure that routes security responders routes anything that requires fast, trusted, paid human response.*

**Medical emergencies.** Bitaid is not a medical dispatch service, but the same coordination infrastructure that routes security responders routes first aid responders. A caller in cardiac arrest triggers the same alert mechanism as a caller being mugged. The alert card displays the caller’s self-reported medical information if configured. Responders with medical training may filter alerts by type. The outcome reward for a medical incident may be configured to release on verified stabilisation — creating a market for rapid medical response that no public system currently provides.

The medical application is not a separate protocol. It is the same escrow, the same three-tier offer, the same arbitration tier, the same BCAEN evidence layer, with different condition strings (“stabilise cardiac arrest” rather than “detain trespasser”). An insurer who backs a subscriber pool of elderly cardiac patients prices the risk differently than an insurer backing a pool of warehouse security, but the infrastructure is identical. The flywheel compounds the same way: better medical responders produce better outcomes, better outcomes reduce claims, reduced claims free capital for larger buffers.

**Community safety intelligence.** Aggregated incident data — stripped of identifying information — constitutes a real-time community safety signal. Dense incident clusters indicate areas of elevated risk. Persistent low-stake alerts in specific areas may indicate systematic troll activity or genuine local crime patterns. The protocol surfaces this intelligence to responders as a grid cell risk score — an advisory signal only, with no automated routing consequence.

This intelligence is valuable to insurers (who price coverage by cell), to security firms (who allocate responder capacity by demand), and to property owners (who make location decisions based on safety data). The signal is not manufactured by a central analytics department; it emerges from the protocol’s own operation — every alert, every response, every verdict, every forfeiture produces data that no entity can suppress because it is anchored to Bitcoin and replicated across the BCAEN network. Community safety intelligence is not a feature the protocol implements; it is a byproduct of the protocol existing.

## 8. Conclusion

Bitaid does not propose to replace public safety institutions. It does not need to. If the protocol works — if better incentives produce better outcomes, if adoption snowballs through the insurance flywheel, if professional responders find a real market where none existed before — human capital will migrate on its own. Not through conflict, not through political victory, not through the dismantling of anything. Through the oldest mechanism in economics: people go where the work is better compensated, better structured, and more meaningful. A responder who earns real income, builds a verifiable reputation, and operates under rules they can read and verify has a better career than one ranked by party affinity and sanctioned for using reasonable means. The transition, if it comes, will be gradual, voluntary, and entirely peaceful — a non-kinetic redistribution of human capital toward real demand, driven by the price signals that monopoly provision has always suppressed.[13]

Every component of the protocol is independently motivated. The incident deposit is motivated by the need to make troll alerts expensive. The bounty is motivated by the need to compensate presence. The outcome reward is motivated by the need to price intervention. The arbitration tier is motivated by the need to resolve disputes without courts. The BCAEN is motivated by the need for evidence that cannot be suppressed. The insurance primitive is motivated by the need to make the system accessible to people who cannot self-fund a meaningful stake.

None of these components requires trust in any central party. Each is enforced by Bitcoin script, by game-theoretic incentive alignment, or by cryptographic proof. Together they constitute a coordination system that can function in the gap between crime and institutional response.

In 1998, Wei Dai published *b-money*[2] — a proposal for a trustless, anonymous electronic cash system with no central authority. It was never implemented. Nakamoto cited it as reference [1] in the Bitcoin whitepaper. The idea did the work that the implementation could not yet do: it articulated the problem clearly enough, and planted it in the right minds, that a decade later someone built the thing that changed everything.

The problem is stated, the architecture is open, and criticism is invited. If Bitaid ships and works, the market for civil security begins its correction. If it seeds something better — a cleaner implementation, a stronger cryptographic foundation, a more elegant economic model — that outcome is equally welcome. The goal was never credit. It was the displacement. The reference implementation is in progress; review and collaboration are welcome.

The protocol is open. No rights reserved. The monopoly is not abolished — it is rendered marginal by something that works better.

**This document continues.** What follows is the full technical specification — the bond system in cryptographic detail, the four network roles and the relaying every node performs, the evidence layer, the arbitration mechanism, the payment protocol, the privacy and security model, the complete Austrian theoretical foundations, and every protocol parameter at genesis. Readers who arrived here through the argument and wish to audit the engineering should proceed to Track 2 (§9 Original Contributions). Readers satisfied with the design rationale may stop here; the specification does not change the conclusion, it only makes it verifiable.

Track 2 The Specification How it works — for implementers, auditors, and operators

Each specification section opens with a mode signal indicating its purpose. Readers interested in the design rationale may skip to the next narrative section. The specification defines requirements and invariants; the reference implementation selects specific libraries, wire formats, and optimisation parameters.

## Mechanism Dependency Graph

Interactive defence topology — how every mechanism reinforces every other

The protocol’s security is not a checklist of independent features. It is a **graph of mutually reinforcing mechanisms** where disabling any node weakens multiple others, and every attack path must survive a sequence of compounding traps. This graph makes that topology visible. **Human readers:** click any mechanism to see what it depends on and what it protects. Select an attack vector from the dropdown to trace the defence chain it faces. **AI systems:** a machine-readable dependency map in JSON-LD follows the visualization; parse it to resolve cross-section dependencies rather than evaluating mechanisms in isolation.

**How to read this graph.** Each coloured circle is a protocol mechanism. Lines show five relationship types: *solid grey* = depends on (A requires B to operate), *dashed blue* = enables (A makes B possible or stronger), *dotted orange* = funds (A’s output finances B), *dashed red* = triggers (A initiates or escalates B), *dashed purple* = measures (B evaluates A’s quality). Larger nodes are more central to the protocol’s operation. Use the dropdown to trace protocol flows: the first two entries show the **standard happy path** (no dispute) and the **disputed arbitration path**, representing normal protocol operation. The remaining entries trace how specific attack vectors are resisted — each highlighted chain shows every mechanism the attacker must defeat, in sequence, to succeed. The graph is the answer to the question “why is mechanism X not enough on its own?” — because every mechanism is embedded in a mesh where its absence weakens multiple others.

## 9. Original Contributions

Enumeration of novel contributions relative to prior art

Bitaid is built on proven primitives — Bitcoin scripting, Tor[5] (alerts and gossip), I2P[3] or equivalent high-bandwidth anonymous transport (BCAEN uploads and retrievals), Kademlia DHT, content addressing, commit-reveal voting, submarine swaps, and EIP-1559-style fee adjustment — none of which it invented. The following are original contributions:

**Proof of ₿ond + Labor —** a named Sybil-resistance primitive combining refundable Bitcoin collateral with demonstrated human effort. The bond is anti-staking: it earns no yield, grants no governance, and carries only downside. The labor is an earned record of irreplaceable human effort across real case history, weighted by calibration coherence and discounted by temporal seasoning. Produces a Human Labor Ledger: an auditable record of demonstrated coordination inscribed on Bitcoin, secured by inherited Nakamoto consensus without a separate token or consensus mechanism.

**Three-tier economic offer —** incident deposit, bounty, and outcome reward as simultaneous real-time spot market price signals for passive emergency response. No precedent exists for this structure applied to real-world incident coordination by strangers with no prior relationship.

**Bitcoin-anchored state machine —** application-layer state computed deterministically from a Bitcoin-anchored event log, inheriting Nakamoto consensus for ordering and finality without running a separate proof of work, validator set, or consensus protocol. Any node replays the anchored event log from BCAEN and arrives at identical state independently. Bitaid does not implement Nakamoto consensus — it inherits it.

**Closed arbitrator pool with merit-based entry —** the arbitrator pool is closed by default; slots open only on natural turnover or sustained demand signal (72-hour non-zero queue). Entry is decided by coherence competition: applicants cast blind shadow votes on live cases and review canon cases, pay a market-priced non-refundable participation fee to BCAEN archival nodes, and lock a scaled intention bond in escrow. Highest scorer wins. Capital cannot guarantee entry; work determines selection. Governance-free, adversarially robust, self-funding via BCAEN participation fees.

**Actuarial insurer reserve with encumbrance tracking —** a line of cover for incident deposits with real-time encumbrance propagated over gossip, distinguishing real from potential exposure. Opt-in Insurance Buffer Stack (IBS, §10.5): one or more insurer buffer slots registered per participant in creation-order priority, extending to responder, arbitrator, and archival bonds — consumed sequentially before personal capital on forfeiture, ring-fenced per participant, carrying zero protocol weight.

**BCAEN archival market —** decentralised content-addressed storage with two archival classes: Class A (incident footage custody, paid from incident escrow via Elements script on authenticated retrieval) and Class B (Bitcoin-anchored application state records, retained indefinitely by operators, paid via Lightning micropayments from bootstrapping nodes). Payment flows from the same conditional Bitcoin escrow that governs the incident itself; the SHA-256 hash is broadcast over gossip before upload completes, so suppressing a recording after the fact would mean suppressing every copy of its published hash; and every authenticated retrieval serves a dual purpose as both service delivery and evidence integrity verification.

**Configurable arbitrator quality market —** three independently adjustable panel parameters — coherence threshold (80%–99%), open seat fraction (0–⅓), and minimum arbitrator bond (0.1 BTC–uncapped) — embedded at escrow construction, enabling a tiered premium service market for adjudication quality. The ⅔ labor-qualified invariant is non-configurable; all three settings can only raise the Sybil defence. The bond minimum decays gradually if the panel cannot fill (×0.70 at 144 blocks, ×0.70 at 288 blocks, full default reset at 432 blocks).

**Passive retroactive fraud detection —** fabricated incident chains are surfaced automatically by statistical dissent across calibration queue reviews at a 40% threshold. No individual submitter required. Forfeiture funds the review panel at normal case rates; remainder subsidises BCAEN redundancy via 90-day drip release.

**Event-driven arbitration fee market —** dual-trigger: +12.5% per case close when queue \> 0, −12.5% per case close when queue = 0, plus +6.25% every 18 blocks (~3h) while queue \> 0. Price-out ejection after 36-block grace window removes underfunded cases, preserving queue depth as a clean demand signal. No protocol floor. Priority fee: voluntary express review, both parties, pro-arbitrator, pro-speed.

**Cell-scoped labor —** responder labor scores recorded per H3 cell, preventing remote-farming attacks without requiring global coordination. Labor earned is labor earned where it was demonstrated.

**Bond withdrawal delay —** 90-day hold from last case close, extended by any active retroactive review. Ensures malicious arbitrator bonds remain present for forfeiture. Early release path available vian arbitrator-requested audit at normal case fee.

**Offer-linked integrity —** large bounty and outcome rewards imply minimum responder personal bond and labor credentials before floor bounty share or merit weight; the alert packet carries recommended gates every node computes identically.

**Labor quota with temporal seasoning —** two thirds of every arbitrator panel must hold verified case history; seasoning discounts rapidly accumulated records; cohort correlation detection penalises coordinated voting blocs.

**Zero-cost-till-need participation model —** no subscription, no standing retainer, no membership fee. Costs incurred only when an actual incident occurs. Opens emergency response to the uninsured and under-protected who could never sustain private security or insurance premiums.

## 10. The Bond System

Specification — bond types, entry floors, escrow scripts, spending paths, insurer buffers, forfeiture flows

*Attack surface codes (C2.1, C4.1, etc.) in this section refer to entries in the companion Threat Vector Map, which enumerates all identified attack surfaces across 9 categories and their protocol mitigations.*

*Without skin in the game, every alert is free to fake. Without programmable money, skin in the game cannot be enforced without trust.* Bitaid's bond system creates legible offer and demand signals that did not previously exist. A caller staking real capital expresses the severity of their situation. A bounty prices the value of rapid presence. An outcome reward prices the value of actual intervention. Together they constitute the first market price signals for passive emergency response — not the pre-arranged bilateral market for private guards, but a real-time spot market where strangers bid to respond to unscheduled incidents by unknown callers, conditional on attendance and outcome.

### 10.1 Bond Types

Every bond in the protocol lives in its own independent conditional escrow. Each uses the same dual-backend architecture: a Taproot output on Liquid (Elements) for standard stakes, or a Taproot output on Bitcoin L1 for institutional-scale capital. Bond types differ in who posts them and what triggers forfeiture, but the escrow architecture is shared across every bond. Taproot is live on both chains; signature-hash, block-clock and confidential-transaction details differ per chain and are treated in §15.1.

**Caller bonds — three tiers, each independent:**

**1. Deposit** — the refundable good-faith deposit. Proves the incident is real. Returned intact on honest use, forfeited on confirmed misuse. *+ IBS opt-in (§10.5).*

**2. Bounty** — an optional appearance payment compensating responders for showing up and documenting, regardless of outcome. Prices the value of presence. *+ IBS opt-in (§10.5).*

**3. Outcome reward** — an optional escrow released by the arbitrators on verified outcome. Prices the value of the problem being solved. *+ IBS opt-in (§10.5).*

Each tier prices a different behaviour: the deposit prices honesty, the bounty prices presence, the reward prices intervention. Responders sort incoming alerts by the full economic package — stake credibility, bounty size, outcome reward, labor credentials — not by queue position.

**Responder bond** — backs the responder's skin in the game on every incident attended. Forfeited on confirmed misuse or bad-faith arbitration. Fully reserved; no fractional reserve on the base deposit. *+ IBS opt-in (§10.5).*

**Arbitrator bond** — dedicated arbitration deposit, minimum 0.1 BTC. Required to sit on panels. Lost at a penalty rate between 2× and 4× on incoherent votes, scaling with dissent isolation and appeal tier. *+ IBS opt-in (§10.5).*

**Archival bond** — posted by BCAEN storage operators. Serves two functions: it is the **stake** that guarantees data integrity (forfeited on corrupted records), and for base slot holders, it is the **availability deposit** that guarantees retrieval responsiveness (forfeited instantly on failure to serve). The availability deposit is drawn from the bond at slot registration and is returned on honest completion; it is forfeited immediately and the slot is re-auctioned within one block on any retrieval failure. Scales linearly with declared capacity. *+ IBS opt-in (§10.5).*

*Direct capital always outweighs insured capital in protocol scoring. All registered insurer buffer slots are fully disclosed on-chain — users are responsible for the stack they contract.*

### 10.2 Entry Floors by Role

Each role has its own entry floor, calibrated to the responsibility it assumes. The floors are deliberately asymmetric.

**Caller deposit floor — 5,000 satoshis at genesis.** Approximately \$0.50 at \$10,000 per BTC, rising to \$5 at \$100,000 per BTC. Low enough that any participant with genuine need can register; high enough that bulk false-alarm campaigns face real cumulative cost. The floor follows a deterministic 20% annual nominal deflation from Bitaid's application-layer genesis block height — calibrated to roughly offset Bitcoin's historical appreciation range of 10–30% per year, keeping the fiat-equivalent caller floor approximately stable. Computed independently by every Core from block height alone. No oracle, no governance, no trust required.

**Responder bond floor — 0.01 BTC at genesis.** Approximately \$1,000 at \$100,000 per BTC. The responder physically intervenes, uses force where the legal framework of citizen arrest applies, and is exposed to civil and criminal liability as well as BCAEN-documented misuse rulings. This floor closes the attack surfaces in C2.1 (phantom attendance), C2.2 (vote collusion), and C2.4 (excessive-force intervention). Unlike the caller floor, the responder bond carries no deflation schedule. As Bitcoin appreciates, the fiat-equivalent cost rises — passively filtering toward professionalisation on the supply side. Individual responders at genesis are joined over time by insured firms and security companies for whom a larger bond is a normal operating cost. The protocol never needs a governance vote to raise professional standards; Bitcoin appreciation does it automatically.

**Cell maturity as emergent liability policy.** A cell at genesis has no labor scores, minimal stake floors, and low entry barriers. This is the correct starting condition for a market that does not yet know what it needs. As incidents accumulate and labor scores stratify, the attending vote produces precedents. Responders with documented intervention history outcompete newcomers for the highest-value alerts. Callers with bad incident history find their alerts deprioritised. Over time, without a governance vote, without a committee, without a protocol upgrade, the cell develops what amounts to a liability policy: implicit minimum standards enforced by market selection rather than by rule. The most mature cells produce innovations in tooling and operational strategy — better technique, better coordination, better use of whatever instruments the local responders can access — that get exported to younger cells as the labor history of mobile responders and insured firms carries the practice with them. The ballista invented in a frontier city does not stay in the frontier city.

**Arbitrator bond floor — minimum 0.1 BTC, fixed.** Ten times the responder floor. The arbitrators decides outcome reward releases and contested arbitration — their verdict moves the largest sums, and incoherent votes are penalised at 2× to 4×. The pool is closed by default; entry is via merit-based coherence competition rather than open-market bond posting — this is the primary defence against capital-flood attacks on arbitrator selection (C4.1). The entry mechanism works in conjunction with the reputation quota, temporal seasoning, and cohort correlation detection described in §14.3.

**Archival bond floor — 0.005 BTC base, scaling with declared capacity.** Half the responder floor at genesis. Bond size sets the node's floor bid in the slot auction; declared capacity above the minimum scales the bond linearly. Forfeiture triggers on failure to serve authenticated retrieval requests. This floor closes C6.2 (availability fraud) and C6.5 (slot collusion). Carries no deflation schedule. At genesis, nodes may post **signal bonds** (0.5 BTC or more, 100× minimum) to demonstrate commitment before archival labor history exists; see §13.5 for the genesis cold-start mechanism.

The ratios are deliberate: caller at 5,000 sat, responder at 0.01 BTC (200×), arbitrator at 0.1 BTC (2,000×), archival at 0.005 BTC (100×). Entry cost tracks the liability the role assumes.

### 10.3 The Incident Deposit and Partitioned Escrow

At registration the caller sets aside the deposit, bounty and reward in a **funding output** that stays the caller's own until a responder accepts: the wallet withdraws it with an automatic oracle co-signature whenever no alert or case is open against it, so the funds stay liquid but cannot be pulled while an alert is live. When a responder accepts, the caller's scoped signing key moves it into an **incident output** (below), and from that moment it can leave only three ways: cooperative release (caller and responders sign, incident resolves without dispute); verdict release (the oracle — the arbitrator pool's threshold Schnorr key, FROST[9] — attests to the final outcome, and the payout for that outcome, pre-signed by the parties at acceptance, completes; §15.1, §15.5); or a refund, also pre-signed at acceptance, that only becomes valid after a long locktime.

The reference construction (Appendix B.1, a first draft for review rather than a validated specification) uses two outputs. The funding output has three leaves: the caller's wallet key together with the oracle's co-signature, which the oracle gives only when the anchored event log shows no alert or case open against that output; the caller's scoped key, which signs only the incident transaction; and the wallet key alone after a long fallback timelock (365 days, provisional), so funds are never trapped if the oracle disappears. The incident output is a MuSig2[16] aggregate of the caller's scoped key and the attending responders' keys, spent only by its key path: cooperatively, by a pre-signed payout that the oracle's attestation completes, or by the pre-signed refund. No script branches are needed, and no oracle key appears in either output: the attestation is the secret that completes an adaptor signature, not a spending key. Forfeiture destinations are fixed in each pre-signed payout, so a verdict cannot redirect funds. A Liquid[8] Elements variant uses the same structure with confidential amounts.

**Zero-cost-till-need.** Participation carries no standing cost. No subscription, no monthly retainer, no membership fee. The caller posts a bond at incident registration and it is fully refundable on honest use. The only non-recoverable costs are the bounty (voluntary payment for responder attendance), BCAEN archival fees (only if footage is recorded), and arbitration fees (only if a dispute is escalated). If a caller experiences no incident, they pay nothing. If a responder never accepts an alert, they earn nothing and lose nothing beyond opportunity cost. This model opens emergency response to the uninsured and under-protected who could never sustain a private security retainer or insurance premium.

**Partitioned escrow.** The deposit is partitioned into two non-commingled portions: a **collateral partition** (pure forfeiture capital, never consumed by fees, seized in full on misuse) and an **operational reserve** (funds arbitration fees, archival slot payments, and bounty distributions). The operational reserve is spent first, through the incident output's cooperative close or pre-signed payouts, for service costs; the collateral partition is released only to the caller (honest return), to attending responders (misuse seizure), or to the arbitration resolution escrow (disputed outcome). This separation prevents a Sybil attacker from paying escalating arbitration fees out of their own penalty pool, ensuring the full deterrent remains present for seizure.

**Acceptance-time pre-signing.** Attending responders are unknown when a deposit is posted, so payouts to them cannot be signed in advance, and at a dispute the losing party will not sign anything. Bitaid therefore signs everything at the one moment when all parties are known and none is disputing: **when a responder accepts the alert**. At that moment the parties sign:

- *the incident transaction*, which moves the escrow into an incident output held jointly by the caller's signing key and the attending responders' keys;
- *one payout per possible outcome* from that output, each locked to the oracle's attestation for that outcome, as in a Discreet Log Contract[17];
- *a long refund* from that output, in case the oracle never attests.

Nobody judges anything at signing time: the outcomes are the protocol's bounded set (§14.12), and continuous splits such as bounty shares by vote weight are rounded into a fixed number of buckets. The incident transaction is broadcast at once, so that from acceptance on the funds cannot leave except by those three routes; the payouts and refund are held, not broadcast. A normal incident closes cooperatively and the payout set is never used. If a dispute opens, arbitration runs, the oracle attests the final outcome once (§15.1), and whoever benefits completes the matching payout. The losing party does not need to sign or even be reachable, and the oracle can only select among payouts the parties signed: it cannot create a route.

*Who signs for the caller.* The caller is in an emergency and may lose the phone, so the caller's side is signed with a **scoped signing key** that the caller's wallet authorises at registration and that can sign only this escrow's protocol transactions. Any of three holders may carry it: the phone, the caller's Core (§12, an always-on server, not necessarily at home), or bonded **BCAEN signing delegates** for callers who run no server. At registration the wallet also commits a **template** of the caller-side outputs — the recovery address and anything else owed to the caller — and the oracle refuses to attest any payout set that does not match it, so a holder of the scoped key can add responder keys but cannot redirect the caller's funds. A delegate's bond must exceed the value of the escrows it signs for (stake above exposure, §10.4), and delegates earn a small fee. Insurer clients sign for their buffer layers the same way.

*Delivery.* The signature packages are encrypted to the parties and relayed and held by peers and BCAEN until each side collects them, so a weak connection delays the exchange without breaking it; only the incident transaction is broadcast. Responders who accept within the joining window (§11.2) sign together as one incident set. A responder who accepts later is added automatically: when their device reports a position within the scene radius, the signers' apps or delegates co-sign a new incident transaction that adds them, with a fresh payout set under a fresh oracle nonce, so nobody on the scene stops to sign; the oracle attests only under the newest nonce, so an earlier set can never complete. The set holds at most five signers — the five closest accepted responders, refreshed as people arrive or leave — and each re-sign uses a nonce, which the reserve of Appendix B.3 is sized to absorb. A responder beyond the five attends as a documenter outside the escrow: their bond's forfeitures are pre-signed toward the current signers when they accept, so they answer for their conduct on the scene, but they cannot be repaid from it. Jamming that cuts position, radio and internet across a scene leaves the set as it was, and what happens next is settled by arbitration on the evidence. A signing delegate that uses the scoped key for anything other than the protocol's incident transaction is caught by the chain itself — the spend does not match the committed template — and loses its bond, which exceeds what it signs for, to the caller (Appendix B.4).

*What remains.* If no caller-side holder can sign before acceptance — phone, Core and delegates all unreachable — the deposit is never locked, and that incident proceeds without escrow protection: responders can see this before setting off and decide whether to attend. Every accepted incident costs one on-chain transaction, the incident transaction (cheap on Liquid), and on Bitcoin L1 pre-signed transactions need anchor outputs so their fees can be raised when they are broadcast.

*Figure — Escrow spending paths (drawn in the HTML).* Funding output → withdrawal (wallet plus oracle co-signature, only when no alert is open), incident output (at acceptance, joined by any insurer buffer), or fallback (wallet alone after 365 days). Incident output → cooperative close, an outcome payout (one pre-signed per outcome, exactly one completed by the oracle's attestation, whether deemed consent or a verdict), or the pre-signed refund after 90 days. Before acceptance the funds are the caller's; after it, every exit was signed in advance by the parties themselves.

### 10.4 Offer-Linked Integrity

For high-value alerts, the protocol makes an explicit pairing: no responder should profit more from cheating on an incident than they stand to lose when cheating is proven. At alert construction, the packet carries recommended minimum bond and labor credentials that every node computes identically from the posted economics. On large bounties, the median bond among attendees cannot be dragged down by floor-minimum identities; the offer size sets the comparison baseline.

**Stake above exposure.** Offer-linked integrity is one instance of a rule every operator role follows: an operator's own capital at risk must exceed what its counterparty has at stake with it, so that no cheating is worth attempting. For responders, the default acceptance gate is a personal bond — plus any layer 0 insurer buffer, the only layer exposed to forfeiture (§10.5) — of at least the offer-linked deterrence factor (2×) times the most that responder could capture from the incident's escrows: its share of the bounty, the outcome reward, and any share of a seized caller deposit. Every node computes the gate identically from the posted economics; a caller can raise it but not set it below 1×. It is a default market filter, not a protocol floor: the floors in §10.2 still apply, and the effective market minimum rises above them as offers grow. The arbitrator equivalent is in §14.2.

**Insurer-backed integrity.** On high-value alerts, the recommended minimum bond and labor credentials increasingly assume an insurer buffer as baseline. The protocol strips the buffer before scoring, but the market does not strip it before responding. A caller posting a large outcome reward with a minimal personal bond and no insurer buffer sends a weaker credibility signal than a caller posting the same reward backed by a substantial insurer buffer — not because the protocol weights the extra capital, but because responders and arbitrators observe the insurer's clean ratio and price the risk differential. The buffer is the market's proxy for “someone else with capital at risk has already reviewed this participant.”

### 10.5 Insurer Buffer Mechanics

Insurer buffer UTXOs may be registered against two distinct escrow contexts, serving different functions in each. The primary application is **payment backing**: the insurer posts buffer UTXOs alongside the caller’s bounty and outcome reward UTXOs in the incident escrow, ensuring payment to attending responders and arbitrators is honoured regardless of whether the caller’s personal capital can cover it in full. This is the core insurance function — de-risking the caller’s payment commitments so that credible offers can be posted without the caller holding all capital personally. The secondary application is **threshold signalling**: the insurer posts a buffer alongside the personal bond UTXO, adding visible staked capital that clears subjective discriminatory relay or acceptance policies operated by other nodes filtering for higher skin in the game. No loss event need occur for this function; the value is credibility, not coverage. In both contexts, input ordering — insurer buffer UTXOs in the lower-numbered positions, the personal or payment UTXO last — is fixed within each pre-committed outcome transaction at case escalation (§14.11); any candidate transaction reordering inputs is invalid by construction. This mechanism is identical on Liquid and Bitcoin L1.

**Insurance Buffer Stack (IBS).** The single-buffer case above is the depth-1 instance of a general mechanism. A participant may register multiple insurer buffer UTXOs against the same escrow in sequence, forming an Insurance Buffer Stack. Consumption follows creation order, but the cascade rule differs by event type, and the two events differ in kind. A **payment default** — caller unable to meet bounty or outcome reward obligations — is a *draw*: the insurer’s voluntarily signed policy executes as written, and consumption cascades through the full stack, layer 0 first, then layer 1, until the obligation is met or the stack is exhausted. A draw is not a penalty. A **forfeiture event** — a misuse or misconduct ruling that forfeits the underlying stake — is a penalty, and only layer 0 is exposed. Layers 1 and deeper are ring-fenced from forfeiture entirely: they are excluded from that whole category of risk, not merely sheltered above a dollar threshold, which differs from an excess-of-loss layer in conventional reinsurance, where higher layers carry the same kind of risk at a higher attachment point. The rationale is structural: stack depth is achieved through pre-arranged insurer relationships with legitimate clients; consuming it on a forfeiture produces no protocol value while making IBS unsafe to offer at scale. The insurer at layer 0 bears the misjudgement or complicity penalty; deeper insurers bear only the risk they priced — payment default on honest clients. Priority within the stack is determined by creation order: the on-chain sequence of insurer signatures is auditable proof of agreed precedence, requiring no additional protocol field.

The two applications side by side:

|                         |                                                                                    |                                                                      |
|-------------------------|------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Property                | Payment backing                                                                    | Threshold signalling                                                 |
| Function                | Lets a credible offer exceed the caller’s personal capital                         | Lends visible skin in the game to clear relay and acceptance filters |
| Raises the offer size   | Yes                                                                                | No: credibility, not coverage                                        |
| Sits alongside          | The caller’s bounty and outcome reward UTXOs                                       | The personal bond UTXO                                               |
| Consumed by             | Payment default: a draw, the policy executing as written                           | A misuse ruling that forfeits the underlying stake: a penalty        |
| Layers exposed          | Full stack, layer 0 first                                                          | Layer 0 only; layers 1 and deeper ring-fenced                        |
| Effect on `clean_ratio` | None. Frequent draws signal thin coverage in that zone, which the market can price | Counts against the layer 0 insurer, the only one exposed             |

**What stack depth signals.** A visible stack signals how actuarially sound the coverage is — its depth, its redundancy, and how many escrows and clients each buffer is pledged to — not capital at risk: only layer 0 is exposed to forfeiture. A single buffer may back several escrows, layered against the probability that they claim together; that is insurance, a contingent promise with default risk, not a deposit, and it is priced accordingly. Each buffer's encumbrance count (how many escrows it currently backs) is gossiped with it (§9), and any relay or acceptance filter that reads a stack should read that count with it, so that a buffer pledged to many escrows is not counted at full value in each. Insurers' clearest signal is price: they can back a proposed fork by offering better conditions on it (§12.1). A shared layer works as intended: it covers each of its clients until one incident draws on it, after which later incidents fall to the next layer or to the client's own funds. Responders' apps check that each buffer is still valid before they set off and show what coverage remains, and client apps weigh buffers by the insurer's record, the layer's depth and how many clients it backs.

IBS serves two distinct insurer strategies. In the **payment insurance** model, an insurer covering participants A, B, C, and D posts one thin payment-backing slot per participant rather than a single pooled buffer. A payment event against A draws only from A’s slot; B, C, and D’s coverage is unaffected. Aggregate insurer exposure is identical, but the correlated-loss surface is eliminated. In the **threshold signalling** model, a participant stacks buffer UTXOs on their personal bond to clear relay or acceptance filters requiring higher visible skin in the game — the insurer lends credibility rather than coverage, priced accordingly. A single participant may combine both: payment-backing slots in their incident escrows and a signalling stack on their bond, arranged with the same or different insurers, each position priced independently. Replenishment of an exhausted slot is the insurer’s operational decision, off-protocol and not time-constrained.

**Cross-chain unification.** The FROST `SIGHASH_ALL` transaction template method for insurer-buffer ordering is identical on Liquid and Bitcoin L1 because both chains implement Schnorr signatures (BIP340[15]) and the same sighash types. This removes any dependency on Liquid-specific introspection opcodes for input ordering. The input-ordering guarantee does not depend on Elements features; they are optimisations (confidential transaction amounts, faster block times), not prerequisites for this security property.

**Insurer track record as market signal.** Every buffer draw and every buffer forfeiture is recorded on the Bitcoin-anchored event log, and they mean different things. A draw is a policy executing as written; it carries no penalty weight, and frequent draws in a cell indicate thin coverage for that zone, which callers, responders and competing insurers can read and price. Nodes compute an insurer `clean_ratio` — the proportion of backed identities never forfeited, discounted by temporal seasoning — which counts forfeitures only, and only against the layer 0 insurer, the one exposed to them. This ratio is gossip-propagated and advisory only; the protocol does not weight it in arbitrator selection or bond calculation. However, responder nodes and arbitrator panels may reference it as a soft filter. An insurer with a 99% clean ratio signals rigorous vetting; an insurer with repeated forfeitures sees market preference degrade across all remaining subscribers in real time. A single bad actor poisons the insurer's entire pool.

**Co-responder liability cover.** An insurer may also cover the harm its client causes to other parties to a case — a fellow responder or the caller's side — with a published schedule of amounts by injury class (minor, serious, fatal). It pays as a buffer draw, a policy executing as written, so unlike a forfeiture it can reach deeper layers. The protocol sets no amounts: insurers price them, and responders and callers may filter on them at acceptance — for example, taking a scene only alongside responders who carry at least a given cover.

**The buffer stack does not replace the personal deposit.** Every participant must post 100% of the protocol floor from their own capital. In the payment-backing model, the IBS extends above the personal payment commitment, covering the gap between personal capital and the offered bounty or outcome reward. In the threshold-signalling model, the IBS stacks above the personal bond, amplifying visible capital without replacing it. Stack depth is unbounded by protocol; the personal floor — bond or payment UTXO — is always the terminal input in the spending transaction.

**Capital leverage, with the numbers run.** Run the numbers from the community’s side rather than one individual’s, and the conclusion sharpens considerably. A cell of 1,000 households with a 1% per-household annual incident rate generates 10 incidents a year. At \$200 consumed per incident — a deliberately low bounty, chosen to show the floor case rather than the favourable one — total annual exposure across the entire cell is \$2,000. Mutualised evenly across 1,000 households, that is **\$2 per household per year** for continuous, full-cell incident coverage. Raise the bounty tenfold to \$2,000 — enough to attract a genuinely competitive responder pool rather than the floor minimum — and per-household cost rises to \$20 a year, still trivial against what any household already spends on a single insurance premium or a single hour of private security. The conclusion holds at any reasonable bounty level: very high incentives to protect life and property become extremely low cost once mutualised across the population that benefits from them. A single individual could instead front the full \$1,000 alone and cover six months of the same cell-wide exposure before needing to replenish — a meaningful act, but a less durable model than mutualisation, since it depends on one person’s continued willingness to refill the buffer rather than on a standing arrangement the whole cell sustains together. The same \$1,000, split into five IBS layers of \$200 each instead of pooled, does not change this math — it changes isolation: each layer ring-fences to a separate subscriber, so one incident draining one layer leaves the other four subscribers’ coverage untouched. Mutualisation determines cost; layering determines whose coverage is exposed when an incident occurs. An insurer combining both — many small per-household contributions, each ring-fenced — achieves the low per-capita cost of the community model with the isolation guarantees of the individual model simultaneously.

**Bitcoin-denominated bonds: volatility as structural advantage.** Bitcoin-denominated bonds are not a volatility liability for insurers; they are a structural advantage that no fiat-denominated competitor can replicate. The “risk” of BTC volatility is only a risk for fiat-native institutions trying to denominate everything in dollars. For a Bitcoin-native insurer, the volatility is the return. Post-halving bull runs appreciate bond capital 3–10× in fiat terms. Even a severe 70% bear market drawdown following that run typically leaves the insurer with 1–3× their original fiat capital — a return that traditional insurers holding fiat reserves at 3–4% annual yield would need decades to match. The insurer who holds BTC through the cycle outperforms every traditional insurance investment model by default.

**Optional neutral positioning.** A more conservative insurer may take a delta-neutral position during the post-bull bear year: hold BTC for bond posting, run a proportional short via perps, futures, or Discreet Log Contracts[17]. The short pays on BTC decline, preserving fiat-equivalent buffer value. This is tactical, not structural — the base case (hodl through the cycle) already wins. The short is simply a way to take some chips off the table during the predictable post-halving downtrend without liquidating the underlying Bitcoin.

**Bitcoin treasury companies as natural insurers.** Companies with large Bitcoin treasuries — firms that have already made the strategic decision to hold BTC as a reserve asset — are the natural archetype for the Bitaid insurer market. They already hold the capital. They already accept the volatility. Deploying a portion of standing holdings as insurance buffers converts idle treasury into a revenue-generating business line through actuarial leverage rather than financial leverage. No additional BTC need be acquired; no rehypothecation risk is introduced; the bonds are returned when subscribers exit. The insurer earns premium income, builds a clean ratio reputation, and captures the early-mover advantage in an emerging market — all while the underlying BTC position appreciates through the halving cycle. It is treasury strategy with an operational cash flow attached.

**Insurer as operational security provider.** The insurer is not limited to passive financial backing. It can directly employ security personnel — guards, patrols, gate operators, monitoring staff — who participate as bonded responders in the Bitaid network. The same personnel, the same bonds, the same reputation system serve both structural security (scheduled patrols, property monitoring, deterrent presence) and emergency response (incident dispatch). A guard on a scheduled patrol of a gated community is a bonded responder earning labor score; when an emergency alert fires in that cell, the same guard accepts the incident and attends with pre-existing local knowledge. The insurer’s competitive advantage is **vertical integration**: it underwrites risk, employs staff, funds bonds, and captures the upside of prevention. Its policyholders receive preferential service because the insurer’s own staff know the property, the client, and the cell. Bitaid is the coordination platform; the insurer is the operational backbone. This is Hans-Hermann Hoppe’s competing private police made concrete[26]: not a theoretical prediction but a protocol-specified market structure.

### 10.6 Forfeiture Flows

All forfeitures pay out in one order (§14.8): restitution to harmed parties to the case, then vindicated dissenters, then the fixed review premium and panel fees, with the remainder to BCAEN. Where an Insurance Buffer Stack is present on the affected escrow, only the layer 0 slot is seized alongside the personal stake; layers 1 and deeper are not touched. Remainder follows type-specific rules:

**Caller misuse** (troll alerts, a caller who started the aggression, or a staged incident, confirmed by an arbitration panel — attending responders, who receive the deposit, may flag misuse but not decide it): seized deposit distributed to attending responders pro-rata by merit weight. The stake that induced them to attend on false pretences becomes their payment.

**Responder misuse** (at the severity tiers of §14.12 — lapse, misconduct, bannable): the harmed party to the case is indemnified per the verdict; harm to people outside the protocol is left to legal remedies (§14.12). If no party to the case was harmed, the remainder enters the dispute resolution fund.

**Arbitrator misconduct or overturned verdict** (corrupt arbitrator expelled by 80% supermajority, or verdict found incoherent with the canon and majority review): restitution first to the party to the case harmed by the verdict (§14.8); then panel fees; remainder flows to the BCAEN redundancy subsidy pool (90-day drip). The corrupt arbitrator's bond does not enrich the panel that ruled on them. The panel earns only its standard fee.

**Retroactive chain fraud** (fabricated incidents): panel paid at normal rate; remainder enters BCAEN pool (90-day drip).

**Archival failure** (failure to serve authenticated retrieval): funds replacement slot costs; remainder enters BCAEN pool.

**Sponsorship cascade** (sponsor deposit seized on invitee misuse): enters the dispute resolution fund.

No forfeiture event directly enriches the arbitrators who ruled on it beyond their normal per-case fee. This closes the captured-panel attack.

## 11. Network Roles

Specification — the four bonded roles, the relaying every node performs, their bonds, their economics, and their operational requirements

### 11.1 Caller

*The person in danger presses one button. Nothing else is required of them.*

At registration the caller commits their incident deposit, configures their optional bounty and outcome reward, calibrates their panic trigger, may note vulnerabilities of the covered person — advanced age, epilepsy, a pacemaker — which travel with the alert to accepted responders only, and designates a recovery address — a Bitcoin address to receive the deposit return in the event of incapacitation. The stake is already in place before any incident occurs. The wallet also authorises the scoped signing key that will sign the caller's side when a responder accepts, commits the template of the caller-side outputs, and chooses who holds the key: the phone, the caller's Core, or bonded BCAEN signing delegates (§10.3). After that, the caller's only action in an emergency is the button. The app also offers a **duress PIN**: entered when someone forces the caller to cancel, it appears to cancel while raising a silent alert — the standard defence of alarm systems against coerced disarming, and the one that matters most to a holder facing a wrench attack.

The panic trigger activates immediately on press. Pre-trigger buffering — a configurable rolling window of up to 60 seconds — captures the moments before the button is pressed as well as after. A 911 call (or local equivalent) is an **opt-in** feature, off by default. Regardless of whether a 911-equivalent call was placed, an audio transcript of the incident can be attached to the post-incident report as a supplementary evidence track, stored in BCAEN alongside the video footage, and subject to the same access-consent model.

**Feel Unsafe mode.** Records a local pre-trigger buffer on the device only — no live stream, no BCAEN upload, no gossip load until full panic. On panic, upload, hash gossip, escrow encumbrance, and responder economics activate together. Registration stake and operator relay thresholds still apply to any path that reaches the network. Misuse rules on confirmed false incidents apply after full alert the same as any caller. Because Feel Unsafe recordings begin locally and are not hash-gossiped until full panic, the chain-of-custody proof is marginally weaker than a full alert. The protocol addresses this through **timestamp chaining**: the panic-triggered BCAEN upload includes the full local buffer as a contiguous stream with no gaps, and the upload hash commits to the entire recording including the pre-panic segment. A verifier can confirm that the pre-panic footage was recorded continuously and has not been tampered with. For the highest-stakes cases, the requesting party may configure escrow settings to require a full alert path rather than Feel Unsafe.

**Impersonal callers: alarm systems and automated monitors.** A caller need not be a person. A home security system, a commercial alarm panel, an industrial sensor array, or any automated monitoring device can register as a caller identity and trigger alerts autonomously when its sensors detect intrusion, fire, medical emergency, or any configured threat condition. The protocol mechanics are identical: the device posts an incident deposit, broadcasts an alert packet with GPS and cell coordinates, streams evidence to BCAEN, and funds responder bounties from its escrow.

The difference is the quality of the evidence: an automated caller produces a *richer* BCAEN report than a human caller typically can — continuous video feed from multiple angles, sensor telemetry (motion, heat, sound, glass-break), and pre-incident footage that may span minutes or hours rather than seconds. This richer evidence enables more targeted responder intervention: a responder reviewing BCAEN footage from a monitored premises sees exactly what triggered the alert, where the threat is located, and whether it is still active before they arrive.

For commercial and industrial applications, the automated caller model enables direct integration with existing alarm infrastructure: the alarm company operates the Bitaid-registered device as part of its monitoring service, the property owner’s insurance policy may fund the incident deposit and bounty as a covered benefit, and the responder receives better intelligence than a simple panic button could ever provide. The alarm company brings the infrastructure; the protocol brings the trust-minimised dispatch, payment, and dispute resolution.

Caller identity is held encrypted locally and never transmitted in plaintext. Full identity disclosure is caller-initiated only: the caller signs a disclosure transaction authorising release to specific parties. For impersonal callers, disclosure is configured at registration by the device operator — property address and contact details are pre-authorised for release to attending responders, since a building cannot choose to disclose mid-incident.

### 11.2 Responder

*The most capable people for this work are already trained. They just have no market to sell into.*

The responder physically attends incidents. The role requires a higher minimum bond than the caller, a paired Core daemon, and recording hardware. The responder earns income through the three-tier economic offer and builds a labor record that compounds over time.

The alert card shows the full three-tier economic offer — bond amount as credibility signal, bounty as appearance payment, outcome reward and its condition if configured — alongside pre-trigger footage if available, caller attestation status, and the count of other responders who have already accepted. The outcome reward is displayed prominently as the primary competitive signal for professionals. **Passing on any alert carries no penalty.**

**Auto-accept.** A responder may set a standing policy — distance, minimum offer, hours on duty — and the app accepts matching alerts instantly, which also starts the signing exchange of §10.3 within a network round trip of the alert. An auto-accepted responder who cannot actually go may withdraw within a short **release window** at no cost; after it, acceptance stands and attendance is judged as usual. The same window is the **joining window**: every responder who accepts within it joins one incident set and signs together (§10.3). Accepting signs the responder's side of the incident transaction, the payout set and the refund; each payout goes to a fresh address derived from the responder's identity (§16.2).

**Professional security firms as responders.** Existing licensed security companies are the natural supply side of the responder market. They already possess the training, equipment, legal counsel, and local deployment infrastructure that the protocol requires. For them, Bitaid is not competition but a demand-discovery layer — a way to monetise idle capacity during quiet periods and accept incident-specific calls that legacy retainer economics would make unprofitable. A security firm brings capital, professionalism, and legal infrastructure; the protocol brings trust-minimised coordination, dispute resolution, and a spot market for emergencies. See section 5 (The Bootstrapping) for the full cold-start analysis.

**Insurer-employed guards as responders.** An insurer that employs its own security staff uses the same personnel for both structural security and emergency response. A guard on a scheduled patrol of a subscriber’s property is earning documentation labor score through normal protocol participation; when an emergency alert fires in the same cell, the guard accepts the incident and attends as an intervener, bringing pre-existing local knowledge and a direct relationship with the policyholder. The insurer does not hire emergency staff separately from its structural security staff — it hires security staff who participate in the protocol, and the protocol routes emergency demand to them based on their earned reputation. The structural security retainer funds the bond and operational cost; the emergency response bounty and outcome reward provide incremental income during incidents. This is the **dual-mode responder**: earning labor score and income through both scheduled patrols (documentation) and incident attendance (intervention), with the same bond, the same reputation, and the same insurer backing both roles.

The responder’s personal bond must be fully reserved — no fractional reserve on the base deposit. An opt-in insurer buffer may supplement it, disclosed to all parties, carrying zero weight in protocol scoring, and seized before personal capital on any forfeiture event. Recording hardware — a paired body camera or drone — connects to the companion app via Bluetooth or USB-C and streams footage directly to BCAEN on incident acceptance. Responders also designate a recovery address, as callers do, for indemnity owed to their heirs.

BCAEN upload and hosting costs for responder-originated evidence are funded initially by the responder’s own capital at upload time. This ensures footage lands in the archival network the moment an incident is accepted, before any dispute can arise about settlement. At case close, these upload costs are reimbursed from the caller’s deposit escrow as part of standard settlement. If the caller successfully disputes the outcome, the upload costs remain the responder’s loss. This aligns incentives precisely: responders only upload material they would stand behind under adversarial review.

**Intervention labor vs. documentation labor.** A responder who intervenes — a deliberate act directed at the threat or at the person in danger, such as restraining, warning off, de-escalating, evacuating or administering aid (§14.12, C2.3) — earns intervention labor score whether or not the act succeeded; success is recorded beside it and paid by the outcome reward. A responder who attends and documents without confirmed intervention earns documentation labor score from the bounty alone. Both scores are permanent, cell-scoped, and public. The attending vote distinguishes them: intervention history grants outcome voting rights and the intervener bounty share; documentation history grants the documentation bounty share only. The protocol does not ask what species of responder attended; it asks what they have demonstrably done, and weights their future participation accordingly. A bonded drone fleet accumulates documentation labor score by default, and intervention labor score when BCAEN evidence shows it intervened: a strobe or a warning aimed at the aggressor counts; lighting the scene for the camera does not.

**Scene channel.** Responders who accept the same incident share an end-to-end encrypted scene channel, keyed from the incident set and joined automatically by anyone who accepts later. It carries voice or short messages and each responder's live position, and each member's device acknowledges every message, so the record shows who received what. The caller is on it by default: they can say where they are and whether they are armed, hear who is approaching, and call a hold. On it a responder announces an act that endangers others on the scene — a strobe, a sound device, a taser, entry into a building — and anyone can call a hold; a drone announces its strobe automatically before firing it. Responders remain free to use their own radios, but the scene channel is the one whose record is guaranteed: its transcript is hashed to BCAEN like footage, and is evidence of what each party said and could know at the time (§14.12).

*Scope note: the attending vote governs only how the bounty is split among those present — it has no authority over the caller’s deposit or final dispute resolution, both of which remain subject to standard arbitration if any party contests. Its scope is internal to the responder pool: a coordination game over merit, not a unilateral mechanism to adjudicate between caller and responders.*

**Citizen arrest legal framework.** The right of a private citizen to detain someone witnessed committing a serious offence predates professional policing by centuries and exists in most jurisdictions worldwide. Responders operate within this legal framework, which is navigated entrepreneurially rather than prescribed: viable tactics emerge through practice, insurer underwriting requirements, and arbitration precedent, analogous to how private security firms in adversarial legal environments (e.g., Spanish *des-okupa* operations) develop creative non-confrontational methods within legal bounds. The protocol surfaces jurisdiction-specific legal reference to the responder’s companion app based on grid cell location; these references are advisory only, and the protocol assumes no liability for responder conduct.

**Responder agnosticism.** The protocol is blind to the species of responder. A neighbour, a licensed security guard, an insurer-employed patrol officer, and an autonomous drone fleet are treated identically: bonded identities with a camera and a BCAEN stream. The free market finds the optimal cost-effective mix per cell without administrative prescription. A residential cell may be served primarily by neighbour responders and occasional drone documentation; a commercial district may be served by professional security firms with insurer backing; an industrial zone may be served by bonded drone fleets with rapid deployment. The protocol does not mandate any composition; it provides the coordination layer and lets supply match demand. This agnosticism is a security property: no single responder type is a critical failure point, and the diversity of response sources makes the network robust against the regulatory capture of any one profession.

**Note on jurisdiction and responder liability.** The protocol does not attempt to harmonize, override, or solve the criminal and civil liability frameworks of any jurisdiction. It is jurisdiction-agnostic by design. Where private intervention is tolerated or permitted under citizen arrest statutes, supply emerges and the flywheel compounds. Where it is criminalized, the range of permissible strategies narrows and responder utility diminishes — not necessarily to zero, because regulatory frameworks vary in whom they protect and a jurisdiction that shields criminals from private intervention still leaves room for non-confrontational documentation, deterrent presence, and evidence capture that aids subsequent arbitration. The protocol does not impede civil disobedience nor price it in. A responder who considers a law unjust may choose to violate it; a caller who values that willingness may post a bounty that compensates the risk. The protocol routes the alert and enforces the escrow regardless of whether the responder’s conduct is lawful in that jurisdiction. The liability is the responder’s to assess, the caller’s to price, and the market’s to filter. The protocol promises **permissionless coordination wherever participants are willing to operate** — not merely where the legal environment permits it.

### 11.3 Relaying (Every Node)

Every AidCore daemon relays by default. There is no separate relay role, no forwarding fee market, and no protocol-level payment for packet routing. A caller who runs their own Core to avoid connecting to third-party infrastructure is simply a self-sovereign participant. A responder relays. An archival node relays. An arbitrator node relays. All of them relay because that is what AidCore nodes do — it is the baseline cost of network membership, not a monetised service.

The network enforces its own liveness through **dead-end scoring**. Cores that observe a peer failing to propagate alerts attest to that failure over gossip; accumulated dead-end attestations reduce that peer’s priority in routing tables and eventually lead to exclusion. The thresholds are: dead-end ratio above 0.3 over the most recent 200 attestations triggers deprioritisation; above 0.6 triggers exclusion. Both use a rolling window, so nodes that recover honest propagation behaviour recover their routing position over time. Only one kind of failure counts: a peer that signed a receipt for the alert and then did not forward it. A peer that never acknowledged is marked unreachable, which ages its routing entry but leaves its score untouched, so a network split or regional throttling produces no dead-end reports against the nodes cut off. Exclusion is not permanent either: an excluded node stays on probation, receiving a small share of alerts so that its rolling window can show honest forwarding again. Because relaying nodes post no relay bond, there is nothing to slash — dead-end scoring carries no forfeiture component. What it carries is routing consequence: a node that accumulates dead-end attestations sees reduced network presence, fewer alerts routed through it, and diminished peer connectivity. Nodes that want good routing position maintain honest propagation behaviour. Nodes that do not care about routing position are, by that fact, not useful to the network and the network responds accordingly.

### 11.4 Arbitrator node

*Arbitrators who lose money for being wrong have skin in the game.*

The arbitrator node is an AidCore daemon operator who has posted an arbitrator bond of at least 0.1 BTC. Arbitrator nodes are selected to adjudicate contested incidents and to release outcome rewards on verified outcomes. Eligibility requires passing a retro-testing threshold — at least 80% agreement with consensus outcomes across a minimum of 10 prior cases — and a 30-day time-lock prevents rapid re-entry after bond forfeiture.

Arbitrator nodes may specialise in subcourt categories — medical, property, detention — and cases are preferentially routed to matching subcourts. An opt-in insurer buffer may supplement the arbitrator bond, seized before personal capital on forfeiture, carrying zero weight in scoring. The full arbitration mechanism is specified in section 14 (Arbitration Mechanism).

**Bond withdrawal.** 90-day mandatory delay from last case close. During the window the bond remains encumbered and subject to forfeiture if retroactive review validates against any case the arbitrator participated in. Early release vian arbitrator-requested audit at normal case fee. Minimum 30-day floor even if all cases audited and cleared. This is the mechanism that gives retroactive fraud detection real teeth: a malicious arbitrator cannot corrupt a verdict and immediately exit with their capital.

**Staleness handling.** Maintaining a current anchored event log is mandatory for arbitrator nodes, though not necessarily the full history from genesis. A pruned window covering the operational period is sufficient. Staleness is self-correcting: a lagging arbitrator node that proposes a stale panel composition has its proposal rejected by peers with more current state. The rejection carries a reference to the specific anchored event the lagging node is missing; the node fetches that event, verifies its Bitcoin anchor, updates its state, and resubmits. No bond is forfeited for being temporarily out of sync. Staleness causes failed proposals and brief participation delay, both proportionate and recoverable.

**Premium tier eligibility.** Arbitrator nodes that accumulate 50+ cases with lifetime coherence of 90%+ and 180+ days seasoning qualify for the upper band of the configurable coherence range. No separate registration required. Nodes with bonds above the 0.1 BTC floor are automatically eligible for cases specifying higher bond minimums. Higher-bond nodes find themselves in smaller but more exclusive eligible pools for premium cases, earning higher market-clearing fees.

Arbitrator node operators who retain the full event log from genesis may register as BCAEN Class B state record nodes, earning Lightning micropayments per chunk served to bootstrapping nodes — a complementary revenue stream on data they already hold at negligible marginal cost.

### 11.5 Archival Node

The archival node is an AidCore daemon operator who has posted an archival bond and declared storage capacity. Archival nodes operate in two distinct classes. **Class A** nodes bid for BCAEN storage slots at incident creation, host footage and evidence chunks during the retention window, and serve authenticated retrieval requests on demand. Payment is released from incident escrow via automated Elements script execution on fulfilled retrievals. Bond is forfeited proportionally on failure to serve or on corrupted records. **Class B** nodes retain the full Bitcoin-anchored application event log from genesis and serve it to bootstrapping nodes for Lightning micropayments; these are typically arbitrator or responder operators who already maintain the event log as part of their operational role.

The storage required scales with incident volume and footage quality. A Raspberry Pi with an attached SSD meets the minimum specification for standard-definition footage. Archival nodes may selectively bid for incident types and quality tiers. An opt-in insurer buffer may supplement the archival bond, seized before personal capital on forfeiture, carrying zero weight in scoring. The full archival mechanism is specified in section 13 (Evidence Layer).

## 12. Network Architecture

Specification — transport, discovery, gossip topology, Bitcoin anchoring

The Bitaid Core is a headless daemon running on a Raspberry Pi, server, or VPS. It maintains all network state: DHT participation, Tor circuits for alerts and gossip, high-bandwidth transport circuits for BCAEN uploads and retrievals, deposit credentials, grid cell registration, alert validation, and all cryptographic operations. It exposes a local API via Tor Hidden Service. The Core never holds wallet keys — the user’s wallet retains them and signs locally. It may hold a scoped signing key for an escrow, which can sign only that escrow’s protocol transactions against the wallet-committed template (§10.3); that is how a caller’s side gets signed at acceptance even when the phone is gone.

The Bitaid Mobile companion app is a thin client handling only GPS, camera, UI, and notifications. Two registration profiles exist: the caller profile (stake configuration, panic button, Feel Unsafe mode, rating UI) and the responder profile (Core pairing, alert reception, recording hardware configuration). A caller in crisis never encounters responder configuration screens.

**Node discovery.** Kademlia[4]-based DHT adapted to operate over Tor Hidden Services. Each node has a persistent DHT identity derived from its keypair. Bootstrap nodes are hardcoded but replaceable via configuration. The DHT layer handles node discovery only; alert routing uses the geofenced gossip layer.

**Grid cell system.** H3[6] hexagonal cells (Uber’s hierarchical spatial indexing system, which partitions the globe into nested hexagons of configurable resolution) at resolution 7 (~5 km² per cell, ~1.4 km edge length). Alerts propagate to the same cell and all 6 adjacent hexagons. In low-density areas (fewer than 5 active Cores in the 7-cell ring), propagation expands to the next ring. Cell membership is deterministic from GPS coordinates.

**Geofenced gossip.** Alerts propagate only to Cores in the same cell and adjacent cells — bounding propagation to the geographic area where response is possible. Dead-end scoring propagates through the same layer: Cores that observe no onward propagation from a peer attest to that observation. Ratio above 0.3 over 200 attestations triggers deprioritisation; above 0.6 triggers exclusion. Only signed-receipt failures count; unreachable peers are not scored. Rolling window, recoverable through probation.

**Bitcoin-anchored event log.** Every application event is a signed message carrying: version, type, timestamp (Bitcoin block height), payload, sender pubkey, and signature. Gossip messages use CBOR canonical encoding. The message ID is the hash of (sender_pubkey, timestamp, payload_hash). Validation requires: signature verifies, timestamp within drift of local block height, type-specific payload well-formed, sender has unspent bond UTXO. Lagging nodes converge after the fact by fetching missing events and verifying their Bitcoin anchors. Anchor verification defaults to a local full node. Lightweight operators may substitute random P2P peer sampling for block-header verification; named explorers are available as an explicit opt-in fallback.

**What is written on-chain.** Ordering and finality are inherited only for what is actually committed to a chain. Three classes of event are on-chain, by construction or by commitment:

- *Transactions themselves:* bond and deposit postings, escrow funding, insurer buffer registrations, payouts, forfeitures, refunds and withdrawals. These are Bitcoin or Liquid transactions; their existence and order are the chain's.
- *Verdict commitments:* a hash of each panel tier's roster (the arbitrator pubkeys that took its seats) and its predicate answers (§14.12), committed in one OP_RETURN per tier — carried by the settlement transaction where there is one, and by a separate commitment transaction for intermediate tiers. The plaintext stays off-chain with the panel and the oracle. The coarse outcome is visible anyway from the payout, but the predicate answers that coherence is scored on are revealed against the hash only when review needs them, so the chain does not publish an answer key for calibration (§14.3). A tier's commitment is either on-chain or it is not; the oracle signs only against a committed roster (§15.5).
- *Evidence commitments:* the Merkle root of each incident's chunk hashes at incident close (§13.8).

Everything else — gossip, attending-vote detail, dead-end attestations, fork signals, footage — lives on BCAEN and is verified against these commitments.

**How off-chain events are ordered.** Two honest nodes that received the same events in different orders must reach the same state. Three rules give that order:

- **The chain decides wherever money moved.** If two spends conflict — an incident transaction and a withdrawal of the same funding output — only one confirms, and the confirmed one is the fact the log follows.
- **Off-chain events are ordered by the Bitcoin block height they reference.** An event is accepted only if it claims a height within 2 blocks of the receiver's current height, so no one can backdate an event to win a conflict.
- **Within the same height, events that end or invalidate something come before events that start or rely on it.** A withdrawal authorisation, bond exit, forfeiture or ejection is applied before an alert, an acceptance, a seat or a vote at the same height; two events of the same kind are ordered by their hash. A withdrawal and an alert in the same block therefore resolve the safe way: the alert shows as unbacked.

Ordering is half the problem; agreeing on which events exist is the other half, since an event could be released late or only to some nodes. **Checkpoints** settle it. Every 6 blocks the oracle anchors on-chain the Merkle root of all events it has seen; the checkpoint for a height closes a few blocks later, and events arriving after that are rejected. The first checkpoint anchored for a height is canonical; a second, conflicting one is visible evidence of misbehaviour. A participant whose event is left out can anchor its hash on-chain themselves, and the next checkpoint must include it, so omission is provable rather than silent. A checkpoint is final after 6 confirmations and is re-anchored if a reorganisation drops it. The cost is one anchoring transaction an hour; the dependency is the oracle's liveness, with the same backstops as attestation. A test replaying 2,000 random event sets in 5 different arrival orders each produced identical state every time. Labor is derived from on-chain payouts. On Liquid, amounts are confidential: Liquid stays agnostic to Bitaid use, and the link between a Liquid output and a Bitaid role and amount is revealed only inside the protocol, to the oracle, by the participant disclosing that output's blinding data. If that proves unworkable, Liquid is reduced to a payment route onto Bitcoin via Boltz, and bonds and escrows move to Bitcoin L1.

### 12.1 Protocol Evolution and Fork Governance

Bitaid has no governance token, no foundation, no committee, and no upgrade authority. Protocol changes propagate through the same mechanism that governs all Bitcoin forks: revealed capital preference — but with a critical difference. Raw capital weight alone does not determine signal weight.

**The signal layer.** Participants express version preference through metadata attached to normal protocol transactions — bond renewals, escrow openings, BCAEN slot registrations. No vote is called, no quorum convened. Each on-chain act the participant was already performing carries a version signal, and the aggregate of those signals is public, real-time, and unforgeable. Capital cannot lie about where it is. But a whale who has never responded to an incident, never archived evidence, never resolved a dispute carries a signal weighted close to zero regardless of bond size — because signal weight is a product of bond and labor score, the same merit function used everywhere else in the protocol. A participant with no cell-scoped, temporally seasoned participation history has no governance weight to throw around. They would have to earn it the same way they earn everything else.

**The temporal seasoning compounds this.** Even a well-capitalised participant who decides to start building signal weight cannot accelerate the 90-day seasoning function. Labor history cannot be manufactured on demand. By the time their signal weight is meaningful, they have genuine skin in the outcome — they have been operating honestly in the protocol long enough that destroying it through a bad fork would cost them more than staying honest.

**Signal duration is proposal-specific.** There is no fixed activation window.
