By the time they reach it, their operation is so large, so old, and so visible that exponential cost growth is merely the mechanism that formalizes their inevitable detection. The attacker’s “success” at each layer does not reduce downstream risk; it **increases** it by forcing the attacker to maintain a larger, longer, more complex deception that leaves more evidence against a system specifically designed to catch inauthenticity. Nothing guarantees detection at any single layer — but dishonesty is more reasonable and easier to catch than to conceal at every layer simultaneously.

**State-actor exhaustion.** The pulsed attack strategy — opening one slot per 432-block cycle and resting between pulses to avoid fee compounding — still requires 2–3 years to place a corrupted majority, because honest turnover simultaneously replenishes the pool, the retroactive review surface grows across the entire fake case history, honest arbitrator coherence histories deepen to hundreds of cases, and the target pool size grows through natural adoption. Before the pulsed strategy can begin, the attacker must first clear layers one through five: build convincing cell-scoped labor in an active cell under continuous honest review, survive retroactive scrutiny, complete 90 days of seasoning, and produce cases that register as queue-weighted pressure — a 90+ day minimum with forfeiture risk on every case, not included in the 2–3 year estimate. The attacker needs a 2–3 year unbroken winning streak across every filter layer simultaneously. The protocol needs any single one of those layers to fail once. Under sustained congestion the time trigger alone multiplies the fee about 790,000× by week 4, about 6×10^11 by week 8, and beyond 10^150 over two years, before counting case closes. The BTC cost at any point depends on the genesis starting fee, provisionally 0.0002 BTC per seat (Appendix B.7). A 51% attack on Bitcoin has a fixed capital cost: buy mining hardware, point at the chain, execute the reorg. This attack has no fixed cost and no hardware to buy: it requires running a convincing emergency response film studio for months while every frame becomes permanent evidence, against arbitrators specifically selected for detecting inauthenticity, on a system that gets stronger every 18 blocks. The defence is not static; it accelerates against the attacker in real time.

**Parametric accounting.** Every estimate of capture cost depends on a small set of parameters, and should be read as a function of them rather than as a point claim: pool size, panel size, per-case review time, arbitrator availability, background case volume, and the slot rate. Some bounds do not depend on any of them. Seats forced open by queue pressure arrive at most one per sustained 432-block (~72 hour) window, so forcing *k* seats that way takes at least *k* windows regardless of budget — 101 seats of a 200-seat pool is not less than roughly 300 days, before seasoning, probation or retroactive review begin. That bound covers only the forced-slot path. The patient path — entering through natural turnover by winning honest coherence competitions, then defecting — is defended by probation, unwindowed coherence history, the 90-day withdrawal delay and retroactive review, not by the slot rate.

**Simpler alternatives and why they fail.** Staked random sampling with long lockup and severe slashing — no reputation, no seasoning, no cohort detection — immediately runs into the 50% control problem: an attacker with more than half the pool by bond weight wins every vote, and the bond floor required to make that acquisition astronomically expensive also excludes every participant who is not already wealthy. You trade mechanism complexity for access exclusion and still do not solve capital capture. Delegating arbitration to a small fixed federation is not an alternative — it is already the formal arbitration path (see §14.11), available to parties who want it. The protocol does not compete with simplicity; it offers trust-minimised coordination as the default and institutional arbitration as the opt-in.

### 14.11 Formal Arbitration Path

For parties in pre-existing commercial relationships — an insurer and subscriber, a security firm and client, a property manager and tenant — a voluntary formal arbitration path adds a federation multisig spending path to the escrow at construction time. The federation consists of credentialed AidCore nodes operated by a recognised arbitration body (ICC, LCIA, UNCITRAL, or equivalent).[20][21] The arbitration body adjudicates off-protocol using the BCAEN evidence as documentary record. This path is only available when all relevant parties share a pre-existing institutional relationship including a recognised arbitration clause.

The federation’s N-of-M pubkeys are embedded as a **fourth Taproot spending path** at escrow construction, alongside the cooperative, verdict, and timeout paths. The chosen institution adjudicates using the same BCAEN evidence the protocol produced, operating under its own independent international jurisdiction rather than under any state’s domestic courts. This is not a security fallback for a failing on-chain mechanism; it is a commercial convenience for parties who already have a contractual relationship with a preferred arbitration venue. The protocol does not compete with formal arbitration; it offers it as an opt-in for parties who want institutional weight alongside cryptographic enforcement.

**Why formal arbitration is not the nation-state defence.** The on-chain arbitration mechanism is economically self-defending against state-level attack through exponential cost compounding (see §14.10). Formal arbitration does not supplement a weak on-chain system; it serves a different clientele entirely — commercial parties with pre-existing contracts and preferred venues. A state actor attempting to corrupt the protocol layer faces costs that exceed any realistic security budget within weeks, while simultaneously subsidising honest arbitrators and archivists with their own attack capital. The defence is not jurisdictional; it is mathematical.

### 14.12 Dispute-Type Verdict Checklists

**Special verdict, not general verdict.** Every mechanism above this point assumes a verdict is available to attest to — the attending vote in §14.1, the blind commit-reveal in §14.4, the pre-committed oracle points each candidate outcome is keyed to at case escalation (§15.1). None of that specifies how a panel, or an attending party, actually arrives at a verdict for a given fact pattern. Two things make this more than a drafting gap. First, the oracle-point mechanism requires the set of possible outcomes to be bounded and enumerated before voting starts; an open-ended holistic judgment call doesn't decompose into a bounded set on its own. Second, §14.2's coherence math assumes arbitrators converge on the same answer often enough that disagreement is informative — which holds far better for a series of narrow factual questions than for one holistic "was this handled properly" judgment. Common-law systems solve the equivalent problem with a *special verdict*: the panel answers a fixed set of factual questions, and the outcome is a deterministic function of the answers, rather than a *general verdict* where the panel picks the outcome directly. What follows applies that structure to the dispute types that recur most.

**The Atomic Verdict Principle.** The unit of verdict is the bonded party — one bond, one verdict instance — not a human body and not a physical asset. A single bonded responder may field more than one asset under one engagement (personnel, a drone, a vehicle, or a combination) and still receives exactly one verdict instance, checked against the aggregate of what that identity fielded; an asset-specific predicate is checked against whichever fielded asset is actually capable of satisfying it, not against presence of any fielded asset. What this does rule out is one verdict trying to cover *multiple separately-bonded* responders at once, which is exactly where an outcome space the oracle-point mechanism can't absorb would come from. Each separately-bonded party gets an independent instance of the relevant checklist below, evaluated against the same shared BCAEN evidence.

Each checklist below is written once and applies at both tiers described in the section introduction: attending parties apply it to each other through itemised ballots (§14.1), self-interested but fast; arbitrators apply the identical predicates on escalation, slower but reading the evidence cold. What differs between tiers is who is answering, not the questions. The same predicates annotate the calibration canon (§14.3), which is what makes coherence measurable.

#### Rules of Evidence

Every predicate below is answered **True**, **False**, **Uncertain** (the sources cover the moment but leave it ambiguous) or **Not captured** (no source covers it). Five rules apply to all of them.

- **What no source captured cannot be found true.** An Uncertain or Not captured answer counts as unproven. Whoever claims payment needs their presence and action captured; whoever accuses needs the misconduct captured. The last column of each table says which way an unproven answer falls.
- **Weight of evidence.** Physical corroboration — an object, an injury, a second knife on the floor — weighs most. Words recorded during the event, such as a responder shouting a warning, count as evidence of what the speaker perceived. Statements made afterwards weigh least and never establish a predicate alone. An arbitrator who answers Uncertain is counted as giving the predicate's default (the last column of each table), so expressing genuine doubt is always safe.
- **Outside sources.** Footage from outside Bitaid, such as CCTV or a bystander's phone, is admissible if it is uploaded to BCAEN within the escalation window with its origin stated. Like other people's words caught on a recording ("he started it"), it can support a finding but cannot establish one alone unless at least one Bitaid source is consistent with it.
- **Perception at the moment.** Wherever a predicate asks what someone believed or perceived — a caller's alarm, a responder's reading of a threat — it is judged by what was observable to that person at that moment, including from positions the cameras did not share, not by the full record a reviewer has afterwards.
- **Findings about non-parties.** The verdict records findings about people outside the protocol — for example, that a restrained person was not the aggressor — even though the protocol pays them nothing, so that the record can support their ordinary legal claims.

#### Caller Misuse — Troll, False Alert or Staged Incident (C1.1)

Not every alert carries a stated incident category — some are a bare location and a distress signal. Q1a exists for that reason: it doesn't itself determine misuse, it determines what standard Q1b is checked against.

| # | Predicate | Evidence source | If unproven |
|---|---|---|---|
| Q1a | Alert specified an incident category, vs. a bare location/distress signal | Alert metadata at transmission | Always captured |
| Q1b | A genuine incident existed at the location and time, judged against the stated category (or any qualifying emergency if Q1a=False) as it was observable to the caller | BCAEN footage, pre-alert buffer, responder recordings | True — the accuser's burden |
| Q2 | Caller had a plausible good-faith basis for believing an emergency existed, even if none is ultimately confirmed | Alert metadata, situational context in footage | True |
| Q3 | Caller has a prior confirmed misuse rating | On-chain record — pattern evidence only, never case-decisive alone | — |
| Q4 | The caller initiated the aggression they then called protection from | Pre-alert buffer, responder footage, admissible outside sources | False |
| Q5 | The incident was staged: coordination between the caller and the "aggressor", or conduct inconsistent with a real emergency | Footage, including the aftermath | False |

**Outcomes.** Q5=True: staged incident — every colluder (the caller, any bonded "aggressor", colluding responders) loses deposit and bonds at the bannable tier, all labor credit from the case is voided along with anything derived from it (queue weight, eligibility, invitations), and honest attendees are paid from the seizure. Q4=True: caller misuse — the deposit is seized and paid to attending responders by merit weight; responders who acted in good faith keep their bounty shares, and the outcome reward still pays if its condition was met. Q1b=False and Q2=False: troll — deposit seized the same way. Q1b=False and Q2=True: good-faith error, no forfeiture, no misuse rating. Otherwise: legitimate, standard settlement.

**Who may decide misuse.** Attending responders receive a seized deposit, so they may flag misuse but not decide it: every misuse, troll or staging finding is made by an arbitration panel. The §14.1 SYBIL_SUSPECT fast-track (3-arbitrator review within 24 blocks) remains the expedited path when responders report no real incident.

#### Responder Classification / Bounty Split (C2.3)

This is what §14.1's attending vote exists to decide, made explicit. Intervention and documentation labor scores are historical — already on the Human Labor Ledger before this incident — and they set each attendee's *voting weight*, not their classification or pay for this case. What the vote (or, on escalation, the arbitrators) determines is who did what at *this* incident.

**What counts as intervention.** An intervention is a deliberate act directed at the threat, or at the person in danger, meant to change what happens. It is classified by the act's function and how it was carried out, not by whether it worked and not by what it risked. The caller is buying the threat stopped: a drone that ends an assault with a strobe from twenty metres has done the same service as a guard who steps in, more cheaply and more safely. Paying for risk would pay for the provider's cost rather than the caller's benefit, and would penalise better tools. Success is paid separately, through the outcome reward. Presence and recording deter too — that is half of what a drone is for — but they remain documentation: an act counts as intervention only when it is aimed at the threat and goes beyond what documenting needs. Lighting the scene for the camera is documentation; aiming a spotlight or strobe into an attacker's eyes, or addressing a warning to him through a loudspeaker, is intervention.

| # | Predicate (per attendee) | Evidence source | If unproven |
|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | This party intervened: a deliberate act directed at the threat or at the person in danger, meant to change what happens — restraint or force, shielding, medical aid, a warning or command addressed to the aggressor, or aiming a light, strobe, sound, taser or weapon at them — whether by a person or a machine. Being present and recording is documentation, even when presence alone deters | Footage, scene channel record, timeline reconstruction | False — the claimant's burden |
| Q2 | This party documented the active incident, or produced footage the ruling relies on as evidence | BCAEN upload record, stream metadata | False |
| Q3 | If Q1=True: the act was a genuine attempt, capable of affecting the threat and actually directed at it, not a token gesture made for credit | Footage, timeline reconstruction | True |
| Q4 | If more than one party intervened and an attendee's intervention share is disputed: which act was key — the one that most changed the threat's course and made the others possible — which others contributed, including acts that repeated an effect but added to it, and which added nothing, such as a strobe fired after the threat was already held | Footage, scene channel record, timeline reconstruction | Contributed |

**Classification.** Q1 and Q3 True: intervener credit, toward the intervener pool and this attendee's intervention labor, whether or not the act succeeded. Q1 True and Q3 False: a token act — no intervener credit, documenter credit if Q2 is True, and fraud under C2.1 Q5 if the act was presented to claim pay. Q1 False, Q2 True: documenter credit only. Both False: no share of either pool. Being an intervener does not excuse how the act was done: force is judged under C2.4 and harm to fellow responders under the checklist after it, and a finding at any severity tier zeroes the share. Where the vote can't reach 80% on an attendee, or any bonded party escalates, the case moves to arbitration on the same predicates.

**Payment.** Intervention pays for the act that served the caller, not for the risk taken. Within the intervener pool the key intervention weighs 2, each other intervention that contributed weighs 1, and an act that added nothing weighs 0 — its author keeps documentation credit if Q2 holds (provisional). Redundancy is not the test: a second strobe from another angle that keeps the attacker disoriented while the guard closes in contributed; one fired after he was pinned did not. Contribution is judged only when someone disputes that attendee's intervention share; otherwise the ballots stand. A genuine attempt that failed keeps its weight in the pool but earns no outcome reward, which pays only for the outcome achieved. Threat suppression outranks the aid it made possible; interventions that were independent, or of the same kind in sequence — CPR, then a defibrillator — weigh equally; where there was no threat to suppress, medical aid is the key intervention. The documentation pool is split equally among everyone who documented, a drone included: each source served its function, and a drone's live picture guides the responders on the ground. Footage of the aftermath earns a share only if the ruling relies on it. A leech — no credit either way — receives nothing.

**Labor.** Intervention labor is earned when Q1 and Q3 are both True; the outcome of each intervention is recorded beside it as a success ratio, so the market can tell a responder who often acts from one who often succeeds.

#### Responder Non-Performance / False Attendance (C2.1)

Not arriving, arriving late, or being stopped by traffic is work not done, not misconduct: it earns no pay and carries no penalty. What this checklist penalises is a false claim.

| # | Predicate | Evidence source | If unproven |
|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | Sustained presence of the responder's fielded asset(s) — personnel, drone, vehicle, or a combination under this one bond — where the incident was unfolding, including a pursuit, for the duration relevant to the claimed contribution, including a pursuit, or continuous observation before a human responder arrives | Multi-source BCAEN cross-validation | False — the claimant's burden |
| Q2 | The contracted action was performed by an asset of this responder capable of it, not merely presence of any fielded asset | Footage, matched to the capability of the asset | False |
| Q3 | The claimed outcome was achieved, judged from the responder's arrival to the template's endpoint | Footage, incident resolution record | False |
| Q4 | If Q3=False: failure was due to external or uncontrollable factors rather than responder fault, including another responder's act | Footage, timeline reconstruction | True |
| Q5 | The responder claimed presence or credit they did not earn — voting in the attending vote as present, uploading footage not their own, reporting a false location, or making a ballot statement the footage blatantly contradicts that would have shifted pay toward its author — shown by sources covering the scene during the claimed window | Multi-source footage, upload provenance, location record | False — the accuser's burden |

**Outcomes.** Q1–Q3 True: full pay. Outcome not achieved through no fault of the responder (Q4=True): pay per the pre-agreed good-faith terms. Through responder fault (Q4=False): that responder's share is halved. Not present or did not perform (Q1 or Q2 False): no pay, no penalty. Q5=True: fraud, at the bannable tier. A drone's continuous presence establishes Q1 for a surveillance-type claim; it doesn't by itself establish Q2 for a claim like medical aid administered — that needs evidence that the human-capable asset of this same bonded responder was the one actually there.

#### Responder Misconduct — Use of Force (C2.4)

This is the highest-stakes dispute type in the system. The predicates operationalise the directionality principle stated at the top of this section — who initiated force, who responded to it — not proportionality against a jurisdiction's use-of-force statute. Each distinct use of force is an *episode* and is judged on its own: the first blows, a strike on a pinned person, a hold that follows. Two rules protect the people who take the risk. **The presumption favours the intervener:** when the footage leaves a question open, the answer is the one that clears the responder. **Penalties need blatant footage:** a predicate below is found against the responder only when the sources make the excess obvious, not when a reviewer would merely have acted differently. Scrutiny falls where the video shows it is due, and only there.

| # | Predicate (per episode) | Evidence source | If unproven |
|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | Force, or a device such as a strobe, sound device, taser or laser, was used against a person | Footage — gates whether the rest applies | False |
| Q2 | When the episode began, the responder reasonably perceived an active threat to a person, or was recovering property from someone seen taking it | Footage reconstructing the responder's vantage point | True — presumption for the intervener |
| Q3a | Force was goal-oriented toward protection or recovery, not gratuitous — a purpose test, not a mandate to hold back against a genuine threat | Footage | True |
| Q3b | Force was aimed at whoever reasonably appeared to be the active threat at that moment, not at a bystander or the protected person | Footage | True |
| Q4 | Resulting injury severity (none / minor / serious / fatal) | Footage, medical record if available | — |
| Q5 | Force continued only until the threat was actually suppressed — not assumed suppressed: a restrained or even handcuffed person can still run, bite, or bring their hands round to the front | Footage | True |
| Q6 | Restraint or detention lasted only while the threat was not fully suppressed, the covered person was not yet safe, or until handover, and ended in release or handover | Footage | True |
| Q7 | In recovering property, force against the person was used only to overcome resistance or a threat | Footage | True |
| Q8 | The intervention put uninvolved people at obvious, avoidable risk | Footage | False |

**Outcomes.** Q1=False: not applicable. All of Q2, Q3a, Q3b, Q5, Q6 and Q7 True and Q8 False: lawful intervention, no penalty. Otherwise the severity tier below applies, read from which predicate failed, Q4 and the footage. Incidental damage to property of people outside the protocol, such as a table knocked over in a pursuit, is left to legal remedies. Where Q4 is serious or fatal the case starts at a larger panel and proceeds up the appeal ladder (§14.5) toward maximum scrutiny rather than settling at three arbitrators. This does not route through §14.11's formal arbitration path, which is opt-in and requires a pre-existing arbitration-clause relationship that will not hold for most incidents.

**Harm to the caller's side.** When a responder's force or device injures the protected person or damages the caller's property, the caller is a harmed party to the case if the act was at fault: misaimed (Q3b False), or used close to a person whose vulnerability the alert noted when it could have waited. Without such a note, an appropriate act aimed at the aggressor carries no fault, whatever the frailty of whoever it startled; the protocol does not make responders answer for every body's limits. Where there is fault, the harm table and the repair order of the next checklist apply, with the caller's key receiving the repair on the covered person's behalf.

#### Harm to Fellow Responders — Scene Coordination (extends C2.4)

Responders at one scene rarely work for the same company, but they share its risks: a strobe that stops an attacker also blinds the guard about to restrain him, a drone flown low obstructs a pursuit, a taser fired across a line of sight hits the wrong person. The protocol does not tell responders how to coordinate; it records whether they did, and penalises harm to each other as C2.4 penalises harm to anyone else. The scene channel (§11.3) is the record. The first question is whether anyone harmed or sabotaged another; how much it matters then depends on intent and urgency. Urgency decides whether coordination was owed at all: an act that could not wait needed no announcement, and one that could wait needed an announcement that actually reached the people it endangered. The same two protections as C2.4 apply — the presumption favours the party who acted, and a finding needs footage or a channel record that makes the failure obvious, not a reviewer's view that the act could have been better timed.

| # | Predicate (per episode, per acting party) | Evidence source | If unproven |
|---|---|---|---|
| Q1 | This party harmed or obstructed another responder — by an act, or by a call on the scene channel such as a false hold: struck, blinded, disoriented, obstructed or endangered them, defeated or delayed their intervention, or degraded their documentation | Footage, scene channel record | False — the accuser's burden |
| Q2 | Sabotage: it was aimed at the other responder, or meant to obstruct them — to take over their role, claim the key intervention or spoil their work. A hold called by someone not within reach of the act, to stop a rival, is obstruction; a good-faith hold that proves overcautious is not | Footage, scene channel record, shared positions | False — the accuser's burden |
| Q3 | The act could not wait: delay would have let the threat cause serious harm | Footage | True — presumption for the party who acted |
| Q4 | If it could wait: it was announced on the scene channel and the affected responder's device acknowledged it, or it was otherwise evident to them, in time to protect themselves or step back. An announcement counts only if it names the act and comes shortly before it (provisionally within 10 seconds); a standing warning does not | Scene channel record and receipts, footage | True |
| Q5 | The acting party could know the affected responder was within reach of the act — from the positions shared on the scene channel or what was visible to them at that moment | Shared positions, footage from the actor's vantage point | False — presumption for the party who acted |
| Q6 | The act was started or continued after a hold called by a responder within reach of it, without the urgency of Q3 | Scene channel record, shared positions, footage | False |
| Q7 | Effect on the affected responder (none / equipment damaged / minor / serious / fatal injury) and on their intervention (none / delayed / defeated — the outcome failed because of the act) | Footage, medical record if available | — |

**Outcomes.** Q1=False: not applicable. Q2=True: sabotage — the right-hand column of the table below. Otherwise the act was a fair risk of a shared scene, and no one is penalised, if it could not wait (Q3), was properly announced or evident (Q4), or the actor could not know the other was within reach (Q5=False); a responder who walks into an announced act bears that risk. If none of these holds, the forfeiture is set by the harm done (Q7) and the fault:

| Harm caused | Negligent | Hold ignored (Q6) | Sabotage (Q2) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Delay, obstruction or degraded footage | 25% | 50% | 100%, excluded |
| Equipment damaged, or the intervention defeated | 50% | 100% | 100%, excluded |
| Minor injury | 50% | 100% | 100%, excluded |
| Serious injury | 100% | 100%, excluded | 100%, excluded |
| Death | 100% | 100%, excluded | 100%, excluded |

Percentages are of the acting responder's combined exposure — personal bond plus the first insurer layer — and, like every fine, are drawn from the first insurer layer first and from the bond for the rest. "Excluded" adds the bannable tier's permanent exclusion and voided labor. The recurrence rule of the severity scale applies to the 25% and 50% cells. A hold is a responder's answer to an announced act — "hold, I'm beside him"; carrying on through it is what Q6 records.

**Repair first.** The affected responder's failure counts as external (C2.1 Q4): they keep their classification and the good-faith terms. The forfeiture repairs before it punishes. The affected responder is a party to the case and is paid from it before anyone else (§14.8), in this order: the outcome-reward share they would have received had their intervention not been defeated, read from the template, then the rest of the seizure. Arbitrators do not appraise equipment or injuries: the harm class read from the footage (Q7) sets the size of the seizure, and the seizure is what the harmed party receives. Vindicated dissenters, the review premium, panel fees and the BCAEN pool take only what remains; arbitration fees are already paid from the incident output when the panel sits. Where the seizure falls short of a serious injury, the at-fault responder's co-responder liability cover pays its scheduled amount as a buffer draw (§10.5). Indemnity for a death goes to the responder's recovery address. The acting party's credit for the act itself stands or falls under C2.3, and any finding zeroes their share. Each responder bond's forfeiture payouts carry one restitution variant per other signer — at most four with five signers — so repair to a fellow responder is a pre-signed route like any other. Staging harm between colluding responders to collect is fraud at the bannable tier.

**Harm caused by the caller.** A caller is not a trained professional, and the deposit is orders of magnitude smaller than a responder's bond, so the harm table does not apply to them. A caller answers inside the protocol only for deliberate or blatantly reckless harm to a responder — firing without identifying the target, for example — and then the whole deposit goes to the harmed responder first. Anything beyond it is for the jurisdiction.

**Reparation is directional, not an indemnity.** The protocol eyeballs harm from the footage and reads its direction; it does not assess damages, and it cannot audit what a responder's equipment was worth or what an injury will cost. What it pays is a signal and an internal incentive: the party at fault loses, the party harmed gains, in days, from money already locked, whether or not a state is able or willing to act. Indemnification proper stays with the jurisdiction: the harmed party keeps every legal claim, the record shows what the protocol already paid, and what a court makes of that is the court's business. Calibrating damages is an unsolved problem outside Bitaid too; Bitaid's claim is only that its internal incentive points the right way.

#### Severity of Responder Penalties

The margin for the hard calls of the field lives in the predicates above — the presumption for the intervener and the blatant standard. Once a finding is made, the penalty follows a short, fixed scale, so that one lapse in a real fight is not treated like cruelty, and every further step remains costly to the person who has already gone too far.

| Tier | What the footage shows | Consequence |
|---|---|---|
| Lapse | A brief excess in or just after a genuine fight — an extra blow in the seconds after the threat was suppressed — with minor or no injury | Loses this case's pay; forfeits 25% of bond and layer 0, taken from the buffer first, escalating with recurrence |
| Misconduct | Repeated or sustained purposeless force, purposeless detention, serious injury from excess, or blatant endangerment of bystanders | 100% of bond and layer 0 |
| Bannable | Cruelty; force against someone clearly not a threat; fraud — false presence or credit, staging; tampered evidence | 100% of bond and layer 0; the identity permanently excluded; labor from the case voided |

**Recurrence.** Like an arbitrator's momentum (§14.4), lapses compound. Each lapse adds 1 to a lapse score L that decays by 2% per accepted incident (half-life about 35 incidents), and the lapse forfeiture is 25% × 2^L, rounded down to a pre-signed fraction: 25% for an isolated lapse, 50% for a second soon after, and 100% — the misconduct tier — for a third. A responder gets a margin, never a free pass, and a pattern is treated as what it is.

**Which assets are taken.** Misconduct and bannable findings drain both the personal bond and the layer 0 insurer buffer: the worst case falls on the person who earned it, and an insurer's layer 0 is its bet on that person's credibility. Policy exclusions between an insurer and its client are off-chain; the insurer prices that bet and recovers under its own contract. Fees — a responder's own and those owed to others — and lapse fines are taken from the first insurer layer before the bond, and only in the amount owed: the rest of the layer is returned to an output with the same buffer script and keeps covering its clients. A misconduct or bannable finding takes the whole personal bond and the whole first insurer layer, which for that kind of seizure acts as a larger base deposit. Deeper layers are never touched.

#### Caller Non-Cooperation (C1.2)

|     |                                                                                                                                                        |                                 |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------|
| \#  | Predicate                                                                                                                                              | Evidence source                 |
| Q1  | Responder arrived within the contracted/reasonable window                                                                                              | Footage timestamp, alert record |
| Q2  | Caller acknowledged/engaged with the responder on arrival                                                                                              | Footage, attending-vote record  |
| Q3  | If Q2=False: a plausible innocent explanation exists (caller incapacitated, situation resolved pre-arrival, unreachable through no fault of their own) | Footage, situational context    |

**Outcomes.** Q2=True: normal cooperation, standard settlement. Q2=False, Q3=True: innocent non-engagement, no penalty, deposit returned, or partial responder compensation for the attempt. Q2=False, Q3=False: non-cooperation confirmed — treated as caller misuse per §14.1 (which names bad-faith non-cooperation alongside troll alerts and fabricated incidents), deposit seized and distributed to attending responders pro-rata by merit weight. Uncertain: escalate.

#### Evidence / Access Disputes (C6.4, C6.6)

Procedural rather than incident-outcome disputes, and closer to cryptographically verifiable than the types above — kept lightweight deliberately, and it isn't clear this dispute type routes through the attending-vote tier at all, since it isn't about incident-day presence.

|     |                                                                                                  |                                                                                        |
|-----|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| \#  | Predicate                                                                                        | Evidence source                                                                        |
| Q1  | Footage/content was accessed without the required signed consent transaction                     | On-chain access log (every access event is recorded per-case, not a standing registry) |
| Q2  | Independent hash/metadata verification confirms uploaded content was fabricated or tampered with | Cryptographic hash mismatch, metadata anomaly                                          |

**Outcomes.** Q1=True: access violation, stake seizure, access privileges revoked. Q2=True: fabrication confirmed, immediate purge, full stake seizure, remainder to BCAEN subsidy pool (§14.8). Neither: no action.

#### From predicates to payouts

Payouts are pre-signed when the alert is accepted (§10.3), so every outcome the checklists above can produce must reduce to a small, fixed set before any dispute exists. It does, because each outcome acts on only three things in the incident output and on fixed fractions of the parties' bonds.

- **Deposit:** returned to the caller net of any arbitration fees the caller owes, or seized and paid to attending responders by merit weight (caller misuse, C1.1; confirmed non-cooperation, C1.2), or seized and paid first to one responder the caller harmed deliberately or with blatant recklessness (§14.12).
- **Outcome reward:** paid, paid at the pre-agreed good-faith share (outcome not achieved through no fault of the responder, C2.1 Q4), or withheld. Callers choose rewards from standard templates, none of which pays for detaining or striking anyone as such — rewards attach to the safety of the covered person or property, so a responder is never paid more for using force than for de-escalating. Each template has an endpoint — handover to emergency services for medical outcomes, property secured from the offender (including when the authorities take custody) for property outcomes, otherwise a fixed time after resolution. A reward is judged from the responder's arrival to that endpoint: harm done before arrival does not fail it, nothing after the endpoint reopens it, and where there were several aggressors it attaches to protecting the covered person, not to catching everyone.
- **Bounty split:** one share vector over the signing responders, in the buckets of Appendix B.6, weighted as C2.3 sets out. A responder with no credit (C2.3), not present (C2.1) or found at any severity tier (C2.4, or harm to fellow responders) gets a zero share; responder fault in the outcome (C2.1 Q4) halves that responder's share.

At five signing responders in 20% steps that is at most 7 × 3 × 126 = 2,646 payouts per incident set (the deposit returned, seized by merit weight, or seized to one of five harmed responders), and far fewer with fewer responders. Everything else acts on bond outputs, each with forfeitures pre-signed when the exposure began (B.1), in fixed fractions rather than amounts set after the fact: **25% and 50%** (lapse forfeitures, taken from the buffer first), **100%** (misconduct and bannable findings, draining bond and layer 0), and **fee reimbursement** (a responder whose rejected objection caused the arbitration repays the caller's fees, from the buffer first). Evidence and access disputes (C6.4, C6.6) act only on archivist and arbitrator bonds, pre-signed at slot registration or seat-taking, and never touch the incident output.

**Arbitration fees.** Fees are paid from the incident output when the panel sits, and charged in the final settlement to the party whose position the ruling rejects: deducted from the caller's returned deposit, or reimbursed to the caller from the responder's bond. When the ruling rejects no one — an escalation required by a split attending vote and decided on its merits — fees stay with the deposit, as the cost of the service the caller bought.

**Uncertain at the last tier.** "Escalate" is not an outcome. When the highest tier a case reaches still cannot resolve a predicate, the predicate's own default applies (the last column of each table): no one is paid for what they could not show, and no one is penalised for what was not shown. Uncertainty is never a ground for seizure.

**Who can be indemnified.** Only parties to the case — the caller and the signing responders, whose keys the payouts were signed to — can be paid from an incident output or a forfeiture. Harm a responder causes to someone outside the protocol, such as a bystander or an aggressor struck without justification, is not paid by the protocol; the forfeiture follows the order of §14.8, and the BCAEN record and the verdict assessment are available to support an ordinary legal claim. Between parties it is different: harm is repaired from the seizure first, as §14.12 sets out for fellow responders and the caller's side.

## 15. Payment Protocol

Specification — Liquid, Boltz, Elements scripts, Lightning, institutional path

Bitaid operates a dual-chain architecture: Liquid for standard stakes and Lightning for micropayments, with Bitcoin L1 for institutional-scale capital. Chain selection is not a design preference; it is a consequence of the payment pattern each role generates.

### 15.1 Liquid and Elements Scripts

Standard stakes — caller deposits below 1 BTC, responder bonds, arbitrator bonds, archival bonds — operate on Liquid, a Bitcoin sidechain with confidential transactions, native asset support, and 1-minute block times. The faster block time matters for arbitration: a arbitration verdict that releases an outcome reward should not require waiting an hour for Bitcoin confirmation. The confidential transaction support matters for privacy: the amount in a bond escrow is not visible to external observers analysing the chain.

Elements Taproot[15] outputs handle the conditional payment flow on Liquid, and the same structure applies on Bitcoin L1 (§15.4). The reference construction is drafted in Appendix B.1; it is a first draft for review, not a validated specification (§15.5).

**Funding output.** Before any incident, the deposit, bounty and reward sit in an output with three leaves: the caller's wallet key plus the oracle's co-signature, given automatically whenever the anchored event log shows no alert or case open against the output; the caller's scoped key, used only to sign the incident transaction when a responder accepts (§10.3); and the wallet key alone after a 365-day fallback (provisional). The check is made against the anchored log, not the mempool, because every node sees the same log but not the same mempool. A caller therefore cannot withdraw while an alert is live. Nor can a caller obtain a co-signed withdrawal in a quiet moment and hold it for later: before releasing its signature, the oracle writes a *withdrawal authorised* event for that output to the anchored log, and from then on the output counts as spent. Any alert it backs shows in responders' apps as unbacked, deterministically from the log, so responders see before setting off that there is no deposit behind it. The order matters — the event first, then the signature — and the oracle then broadcasts the withdrawal itself. Before setting off, a responder's app also checks that the incident transaction is in the mempool with no competing spend. The only remaining window is gossip delay: in the seconds before the event reaches a responder's Core, an alert on a just-withdrawn output can still be accepted, and the mempool check catches the competing spend. Withdrawals therefore depend on the oracle being online, bounded by the 365-day fallback; the co-signature itself is free, and serving it is part of pool membership. Callers mostly leave their deposit in place as standing self-insurance, so withdrawals are occasional, and a threshold of members can sign them in batches without the whole pool online. Bonds for responders, arbitrators, archivists and signing delegates use a different output (Appendix B.1): a leaf requiring both the operator's scoped key and the oracle's co-signature, so a bond can move only by a withdrawal the oracle approves after the hold, or by a forfeiture the operator pre-signed when it took on the exposure; and a fallback leaf that returns the bond to the operator's wallet alone after a long absolute timelock (365 days at genesis, provisional), so a bond is never trapped if the oracle disappears.

**Incident output.** Created by the incident transaction at acceptance, it is a MuSig2 aggregate of the caller's scoped key and the attending responders' keys, spent only by key path. *Cooperative release:* all signers sign the settlement the attending vote produced; no arbitration, no fee market, no delay — the most common path. *Verdict release:* each possible outcome has a payout the parties pre-signed as an adaptor signature, locked to the oracle's attestation point for that outcome; when the oracle attests the final outcome, the matching payout can be completed and broadcast by anyone, and no other can. The oracle — a threshold Schnorr key held by the seasoned arbitrator pool (§15.4, §15.5) — signs one message per case: the final outcome, published only after the last re-roll window closes, under a nonce generated for that case alone and committed at acceptance. It never signs a payout transaction and cannot create an output; it can at worst attest the wrong outcome among the pre-signed ones, which is what panels, review and restitution exist to catch. This is the path through which asymmetric penalties, forfeiture flows, restitution and appeal subsidies execute. *Refund:* a payout returning each party's contribution, pre-signed at acceptance with a locktime of 90 days (provisional: about 129,600 Liquid blocks or 12,960 Bitcoin L1 blocks), so funds are never trapped if the oracle never attests.

No custodian chooses between these paths: before acceptance the funds are the caller's, and after it every exit was signed in advance by the parties themselves.

**Clock convention.** Two kinds of time appear in this specification. *Script timelocks* (CLTV and CSV) are evaluated by the chain the output lives on, in that chain's own blocks: about one minute on Liquid, about ten on Bitcoin L1. They are stated here in wall-clock time with the block count per chain. *Protocol windows* — the fee-market triggers, the 432-block slot-opening window, bond-minimum decay, the price-out grace window — are counted in Bitcoin block height, the clock of the anchored event log (§12), at about ten minutes per block, and are enforced by every node replaying that log rather than by a script opcode. A Liquid script cannot read Bitcoin's height, so script-enforced paths do not depend on protocol windows; where the two must line up, the script carries its own chain's block count for the same wall-clock duration.

**One attestation per case.** Panels and re-rolls (§14.5) vote by blind commit-reveal and record their results in the anchored log; none of them signs anything that moves funds. The oracle attests once, after the last re-roll window has closed, to the outcome the final tier decided. Attesting once matters for safety as well as simplicity: a Schnorr nonce that signs two different messages reveals the private key, so each case gets its own nonce, generated in advance by the pool in batches and used exactly once. The 48-hour embargo (§14.1) is therefore simply the period before the oracle attests; there is no earlier attestation to supersede and no race to broadcast. The same threshold key is used on both chains; signing uses a FROST variant robust to absent signers (§15.4), and attestations can be batched per epoch to reduce load.

**Scope.** Pre-committed outcome transactions guarantee a verdict pays out exactly as stated; they guarantee nothing about whether the verdict itself is honest. A bribed or coerced panel attests falsely with the same ease regardless of execution mechanism. That risk is carried entirely by closed-pool entry via coherence competition (§14.2–14.3), blind commit-reveal voting and asymmetric penalty (§14.4), and 40%-dissent retroactive review by the 100-arbitrator pool (§14.3) — mechanisms operating one layer above execution, on judgment rather than settlement. The outcome set must be bounded and enumerable when the alert is accepted; arbitration scope is already restricted to a small set of verdict buckets — directionality of aggression, contract fulfilment, the standard asymmetric-penalty tiers — so this formalises a structure the protocol's verdict categories already imply.

### 15.2 Boltz Submarine Swaps

A Boltz[10] submarine swap backend handles on-the-fly conversion between Bitcoin, Liquid, and Lightning. This is critical for responder and arbitrator UX: a responder who wants to receive payment in Lightning for instant access does not need the caller to post a Lightning invoice. The caller posts the bond in Liquid (or Bitcoin L1 for large amounts); the swap backend converts to Lightning at resolution time. The responder designates their preferred receiving chain at registration; the protocol handles conversion transparently.

The swap is atomic: either the conversion completes and the responder receives funds on their preferred chain, or it fails and funds revert to the escrow for retry. There is no counterparty risk beyond the swap backend itself, which operates as a market maker with publicly auditable reserves. A compromised swap backend cannot steal escrow funds — it can only fail to execute the conversion, in which case funds remain in the escrow and the parties can retry or route through an alternative backend.

### 15.3 Lightning Integration

Lightning is used for payment patterns that require high frequency and low value: Class B archival micropayments (per-chunk retrieval fees paid by bootstrapping nodes), third-party Core connection fees (paid by thin clients to full Cores for routing and validation services), and BCAEN retrieval fees (paid by arbitrators and auditors pulling evidence for review). Lightning is *not* used for: incident escrow (amounts are too large for channel capacity and require on-chain finality), arbitration fees (require conditional release via script, not preimage revelation), or bond posting (requires on-chain locking visible to all nodes for validation).

The separation is deliberate. Lightning excels at micropayments where trustless finality is less critical than speed and cost. On-chain settlement excels at large-value transfers where finality is paramount. Bitaid uses each for what it does best.

### 15.4 Institutional Path (Bitcoin L1)

Institutional stakes — bonds above 1 BTC, typically posted by security firms, insurers, or high-net-worth individuals — operate on Bitcoin L1 with Taproot outputs. The same funding, incident and bond outputs apply (Appendix B.1), with anchor outputs on every pre-signed transaction so its fee can be raised when it is broadcast. Taproot is required: MuSig2 aggregation, adaptor signatures and the oracle's threshold signatures all produce BIP340 Schnorr signatures, which SegWit v0 (P2WSH) scripts cannot verify. MuSig2 aggregates N-of-N and serves wherever every signer is known: the incident output, signed at acceptance. The oracle's key is a threshold key (FROST), because its holders are a majority of a changing pool; panels themselves decide by ⅔ supermajority (§14.5).

Every pre-signed transaction commits to its exact inputs and outputs via `SIGHASH_ALL`, so nothing can be modified after signing — including the order of insurer buffer inputs in the incident transaction, which enforces buffer ordering (§10.5). The oracle's key uses the BIP340-compatible FROST variant (draft BIP, bitcoin/bips #2070, adapted from RFC 9591, whose own keys are not BIP340 x-only keys), with identifiable abort — a cheating participant in a signing session is pinpointed and excluded — and preprocessing to separate expensive offline nonce generation from the online signing phase. The oracle co-signs bond withdrawals and forfeitures as ordinary FROST signatures; its outcome attestations use the per-case nonces of Appendix B.3.

The institutional path exists because large bond holders may prefer the settlement finality and auditability of Bitcoin L1 over the convenience of Liquid. The protocol does not preference one chain over the other; both are fully supported and interoperable through Boltz swaps.

### 15.5 Open Engineering Problems

The sections above state what the escrow scripts must achieve. They are not yet a validated script-level specification, and the problems below are open. None is believed unsolvable with existing Bitcoin and Liquid primitives; none is solved by this document.

**1. Oracle key at pool scale.** The verdict path no longer needs anyone to sign at a dispute: payouts are pre-signed at acceptance (§10.3) and the oracle only attests. What remains is the oracle's own key. It is held by a threshold of about a majority of the seasoned pool, each member signing only if a deterministic replay of the anchored log confirms that the outcome follows from the recorded votes and roster. Open questions: distributed key generation and proactive resharing as members join and leave without changing the public key (D-FROST, IACR ePrint 2024/896, is the starting point); signing with hundreds of participants, some absent (a ROAST-style wrapper, and batching attestations per epoch); and common-mode failure, since one client flaw that exposes shares exposes them all, which argues for isolated share storage, frequent refresh and no silent updates. Because the oracle can only choose among pre-signed payouts, a captured majority can push wrong verdicts case by case but cannot sweep open escrows.

**2. Per-case nonces.** Each case's attestation nonce must be generated jointly by the pool before the alert is accepted, committed with the case, and used exactly once. Appendix B.3 drafts the construction: batched joint generation each epoch with a reserve well above peak load, refilled as soon as half is used, reshared with the key at epoch boundaries, each nonce used for exactly one message. It needs cryptographic review before any funds depend on it.

**3. Availability attestation.** BCAEN’s availability forfeiture (§13) requires someone to attest that an authenticated retrieval failed. Appendix B.5 drafts the procedure: a challenge bond, five randomly drawn arbitrators sending ordinary anonymous retrieval requests, forfeiture only if at least four fail to retrieve.

**4. Per-chain semantics.** Signature hashes, confidential-transaction commitments and block clocks differ between Liquid and Bitcoin L1. Every construction has to be specified and tested per chain (see the clock convention in §15.1).

**5. Threshold key lifecycle.** Distributed key generation and refresh as arbitrators join and leave, and how those operations interact with cases that are already open.

**6. Signing at acceptance.** Appendix B drafts the outputs, transactions and message formats for the pre-signed set (§10.3), the bucket sizes for continuous splits, and the delegate rules. What it still needs is per-chain test vectors, benchmarks of how long a phone takes to produce the payout set, and adversarial testing. Two residuals are accepted rather than solved: an incident where no caller-side holder can sign before acceptance proceeds without escrow protection, and every accepted incident costs one on-chain transaction.

**Acceptance criterion.** These constructions count as solved when they have been implemented on testnet and exercised adversarially — every path terminates, no funds can be trapped, no party can spend unilaterally, behaviour under signer dropout is defined — not when they have been described.

**Fallback.** If threshold-attested verdict release cannot be made to meet these criteria, the design degrades to pre-accepted third-party arbitrators holding federated multisig custody — the opt-in path of §14.11 generalised. That is simpler and easier to audit, but if the arbitrators are identifiable it is less resistant to compulsion. The pseudonymous pool is the harder version of the problem and the one this protocol is designed to solve.

## 16. Privacy & Security

Specification — caller anonymity, responder exposure, device security, state resistance

*Attack surface codes in this section refer to entries in the companion Threat Vector Map.*

### 16.1 Caller Privacy

Caller identity is held encrypted locally and never transmitted in plaintext. The alert contains a pseudonymous identifier derived from the deposit UTXO — sufficient to verify bond validity without revealing identity. Full identity disclosure is caller-initiated only: if the caller chooses to disclose to attending responders or to a legal authority, they sign a disclosure transaction. The protocol does not automatically disclose caller identity to any party, including law enforcement.

Location is revealed in two stages. Initial alert propagation carries only the grid cell identifier (a ~5 km² cell). Precise GPS coordinates are embedded in signed alert packets and shared only with responders who have explicitly accepted the alert — never broadcast to the full cell. The companion app never transmits raw GPS coordinates to any server. All location data is routed only over Tor.

Feel Unsafe mode provides the strongest privacy guarantee: recording is local-only with no network activity until full panic. No live stream, no BCAEN upload, no gossip load, no metadata leakage. On panic, the full buffered footage uploads with timestamp chaining that commits the pre-panic segment to the same cryptographic evidence standard as the post-panic footage. There is no KYC, no registration database, no identity verification, no phone number required, no email address collected.

**BCAEN privacy properties.** Footage uploaded to BCAEN is content-addressed: the retrieval key is the hash of the content itself. There is no index, no search, no directory. An attacker who knows a content hash can retrieve the corresponding footage; an attacker who does not know the hash cannot enumerate stored content. The hash is gossiped at upload time, so all nodes in the propagation cell learn it, but nodes outside the cell do not. This creates a natural geographic privacy boundary: footage is retrievable by anyone with the hash, but the hash is only known to participants in the incident cell. Arbitrators reviewing cases receive the hash through the arbitrator assignment protocol, not through public indexing.

### 16.2 Responder Privacy

Responder identity follows the same pseudonymity model. The responder’s Core keypair is the only persistent identifier. Earnings are received at a fresh address for each case, derived from the responder's identity, so outsiders cannot link payouts across cases; the responder proves each payout is theirs by signing with the identity key, and labor is recorded against the pseudonym rather than against addresses. Coinjoining after receipt therefore costs nothing in labor. Nothing here is linked to any real-world identity by the protocol. Responders who choose to build public market standing may voluntarily link their pseudonymous identity to a verifiable credential, but this is never required.

Responders operate pseudonymously but with higher exposure than callers: they physically attend incidents and their body camera footage is archived on BCAEN. The responder’s Core runs on a dedicated device separate from their personal phone. The responder’s bond UTXO is not linked to their identity by any protocol mechanism, but physical attendance creates correlation risk that responders manage through operational discipline.

The critical distinction is between **alert-layer anonymity** and **footage-layer correlation risk**. At the alert layer, the responder is a pseudonymous keypair accepting an encrypted alert over Tor — no IP exposure, no identity linkage. At the footage layer, the responder’s body camera captures their presence at a specific location at a specific time, and that footage is hashed, gossiped, and archived. The footage does not identify the responder (no name, no credential, no biometric), but it creates a physical presence record that correlates with the pseudonymous identity that accepted the alert. A responder who accepts many alerts in the same cell builds a physical presence pattern that could theoretically be correlated with other data sources. This is not a protocol vulnerability; it is an inherent property of physical response work. Responders manage it the same way journalists manage source protection: dedicated devices, operational compartmentalisation, and awareness that physical presence is the one layer cryptography cannot protect.

**Correlation attack analysis.** A state-level adversary with access to cellular tower records, CCTV footage, and financial transaction data could theoretically correlate a responder’s pseudonymous identity with their real identity by matching physical presence patterns. The protocol mitigates this through: (1) pseudonymous keypairs with no linkage to phone numbers or financial accounts, (2) Tor routing obscuring IP addresses, (3) Lightning[7] micropayments creating no on-chain transaction graph, (4) cell-based alert propagation limiting geographic exposure to a seven-cell area roughly 4 km in radius, and (5) the option for responders to use multiple pseudonymous identities across different cells. A responder who rotates pseudonyms every 30 cases and operates in multiple cells produces no single identity with enough physical presence data to correlate. This is operational discipline, not protocol enforcement — the protocol provides the tools; the responder uses them.

### 16.3 Device Security

**Keys live in the participant's wallet, not in the app.** The companion app is not a wallet and holds no keys. It builds alerts, escrows and transactions and requests signatures from whatever wallet the participant uses — a software wallet, a hardware signer, or a custodial or multi-party setup. Most wallets suffice for a caller placing small deposits. Responders, arbitrators and institutions locking meaningful bonds should use a safer setup than a hot wallet; that choice and its cost are theirs, and the protocol neither mandates nor prices it. Device biometrics can gate the app's own actions, such as creating an alert or approving a signing request, but they protect the app, not the key. Recovery is the wallet's own backup, held offline by the user; the caller's recovery key for the timeout-return path is likewise held in the wallet.

The Core daemon never holds wallet keys — all wallet signing occurs in the user’s wallet. The only key a Core may hold is an escrow's scoped signing key (§10.3), which can sign nothing outside the wallet-committed template, so a compromised Core cannot redirect funds. The Core maintains network state (DHT, gossip, BCAEN connections) but all transaction signing requires the private key that lives only in the user’s wallet. If the Core is compromised, the attacker learns network topology and gossip contents but cannot spend bonds, cannot create alerts, and cannot produce verdict attestations.

**GPS spoofing mitigation.** Multiple independent recorders at the same scene with consistent GPS metadata make coordinated spoofing expensive. A single responder could spoof their location, but spoofing the location of three independent responders plus the caller while producing consistent BCAEN footage is a qualitatively harder attack. Hardware attestation — GPS modules with cryptographic signing, tamper-evident body cameras — is a market-driven response. Insurers who require attested hardware for buffer coverage create economic pressure toward adoption without protocol-level enforcement. The protocol does not mandate attestation; the market prices the risk differential between attested and unattested responders. Over time, high-value alerts will increasingly attract attested responders because insurers competing on clean ratio will demand attestation as a condition of buffer coverage, and callers posting large bounties will prefer responders whose hardware is attested.

### 16.4 State Resistance

The protocol is designed to remain functional under adversarial state conditions. Tor[5] routing obscures participant IP addresses for alert and gossip traffic. BCAEN uploads and retrievals are routed over a high-bandwidth anonymous transport (I2P[3] or equivalent) that has no exit node architecture, removing the exit node surveillance surface that Tor retains. DHT-based discovery has no central bootstrap dependency — bootstrap nodes are hardcoded but replaceable via configuration, and the DHT itself continues to function as long as any subset of nodes remains connected.

On Bitcoin L1, bonds cannot be seized without compromising the participant’s private key, and there is no intermediary holding funds that can be court-ordered to freeze. Stakes on Liquid additionally depend on its federation, an identifiable set of functionaries (11 of 15 sign blocks and peg-outs); Liquid is used as a settlement layer that does not see Bitaid roles (§12), and participants for whom that dependency is unacceptable can hold bonds on Bitcoin L1. The protocol does not maintain any server infrastructure that can be subpoenaed for logs, raided for hardware, or shut down by court order. There is no company, no foundation, no CEO, no board of directors, no registered address. The reference implementation is open-source; the specification is public; the rules are enforced by Bitcoin script and game theory, not by any entity that can be threatened, bribed, or captured.

Relay operators and responder firms are independent businesses in their respective jurisdictions. A state can regulate or prohibit a specific operator within its territory, but it cannot prohibit the protocol itself because the protocol has no jurisdictional presence. It is a set of rules and a reference implementation, not a legal entity. The same structural property that makes BitTorrent resistant to copyright enforcement makes Bitaid resistant to jurisdictional prohibition: the protocol specification is speech, the reference implementation is speech, and the network is a decentralised peer-to-peer graph with no centre to attack.

**Jurisdiction shopping for relay operators.** Because relay operators are independent businesses, they naturally gravitate toward jurisdictions with favourable regulatory environments. A relay operator in a jurisdiction that prohibits Bitaid can simply relocate their servers to a jurisdiction that does not. The protocol has no dependency on any specific operator; if one exits, the DHT routes around them. Over time, the network of relay operators will concentrate in jurisdictions with strong free-speech protections, strong property-rights enforcement, and weak regulatory capture — not because the protocol enforces this, but because market selection favours operators in jurisdictions where they are least likely to be shut down. This is the same mechanism that concentrated Bitcoin mining in jurisdictions with cheap, stranded energy: market participants follow the economic incentives, and the protocol does not care where they are.

**The infancy window.** The protocol does not need a jurisdiction that has declared it legal. It needs a window in which it has not yet become a target — the same default condition that let Bitcoin's early proof-of-work network exist for years beneath the notice of anyone capable of stopping it. Legislators do not regulate what they have not yet recognised as a threat. Obscurity, low initial impact, and irrelevance to existing power structures are not a strategy to be wished for; they are the default state of anything genuinely new, and that default state is what lets a protocol take root, accumulate case history, and set precedent before it becomes visible enough to provoke an organised response. Genesis cells form first in low-friction environments — not because those places have declared Bitaid legal, but because friction there is lowest, and low friction is what determines how long the window of obscurity lasts before scrutiny arrives.

**Regulatory terraforming.** The protocol does not need to be legal everywhere; it needs to be viable somewhere. History provides ample precedent for services that operate in legal gray zones and expand outward as demand compels regulatory adaptation. In Spain, private eviction-recovery firms (*desokupa*) developed creative non-confrontational methods within legal bounds to serve demand that the state could not meet. Uber did not ask permission to redefine transportation in every jurisdiction; it built a system so undeniably useful that regulators were forced to adapt after the fact. The same dynamic applies: where state provision fails and private demand is non-negotiable, the market finds a way. Bitaid cells self-organise where participants are willing to operate; successful cells generate case history, calibration coherence, and insurer participation that make them economically indispensable. Regulators facing a functioning, life-saving coordination network with institutional insurer backing are not choosing between "permit" and "prohibit" — they are choosing between "adapt" and "drive it underground." The former is historically more common than the latter when the service is genuinely valuable and the network has no centre to raid.

### 16.5 Legal Exposure

Bitaid is designed to work regardless of the jurisdiction around it, which is not the same as being exempt from it. The protocol promises no immunity from local law: a responder who detains, strikes or pursues someone answers for it where they stand, and the evidence the protocol keeps can serve their defence or their accuser. Four exposures are worth stating plainly.

- **Intervention law.** Citizen detention, self-defence and defence of others differ by jurisdiction. The checklists of §14.12 judge conduct by the non-aggression principle, not by any statute; a responder who meets them can still face a local charge, and one who fails them can still be acquitted. Responders, and the insurers who back them, carry that difference; insurers backing professional responders can bundle legal-defence cover, as security firms' insurers already do.
- **Unlawful content.** BCAEN stores only uploads tied to a bonded, live incident, which limits abuse but cannot exclude it. Content an arbitration panel finds unlawful is purged by the same path as fabricated content (C6.6), and an archival node may refuse to host it.
- **Targeting innocent people.** A caller can send responders after someone who has done nothing. The caller's deposit and misuse rating are the deterrent (C1.1); the target's remedy is an ordinary legal claim, supported by the BCAEN record and the verdict's findings about non-parties (§14.12).
- **Capital and insurance.** Bonds carry no KYC by design. Insurers that post buffers operate under their own licences and compliance, as a business layer above the protocol, not as part of it.

## Frequently Asked Questions

Common external objections and the protocol’s built-in rebuttals

**Q: How does a cell start with zero responders and zero history? Isn’t this a chicken-and-egg problem?**

A: No, because the market is demand-pull, not supply-push. Callers with skin in the game are the price signal. Responders follow the price; they are not pre-built infrastructure waiting for demand to arrive. Genesis does not require a professional supply chain. It requires a single caller willing to post a stake and a bounty, and a single person willing to pick it up — a neighbourhood-watch interaction at the protocol level. It costs the caller nothing until claimed — withdrawing needs only the oracle's free co-signature — minus transaction fees that are irrelevant at the scale of any meaningful bounty. The sophistication of the supply side grows with the cell: today’s single responder with a phone camera is tomorrow’s insured security firm, once the bounty history makes the opportunity undeniable. The protocol does not build the market. It makes the price visible, and visible prices attract supply on their own schedule.

**Q: What about jurisdictions where private intervention is legally restricted?**

A: The protocol is jurisdiction-agnostic by design. It does not attempt to harmonise, override, or solve the criminal and civil liability frameworks of any jurisdiction. Where private intervention is tolerated or permitted under citizen arrest statutes, supply emerges and the flywheel compounds. Where it is criminalised, the range of permissible strategies narrows and responder utility diminishes — but not necessarily to zero, because regulatory frameworks vary in whom they protect and a jurisdiction that shields criminals from private intervention still leaves room for non-confrontational documentation, deterrent presence, and evidence capture. The protocol routes the alert and enforces the escrow regardless of whether the responder’s conduct is lawful in that jurisdiction. The liability is the responder’s to assess, the caller’s to price, and the market’s to filter. Furthermore, regulatory terraforming is a demonstrated phenomenon: services that are genuinely valuable and have no centre to raid tend to compel regulatory adaptation rather than suppression (see §16.4).

**Q: Isn’t the UX too complex for a crisis situation?**

A: All cryptographic complexity is front-loaded during calm registration. During a crisis, the UX is a single button press. The heavy lifting — escrow construction, bond verification, DHT routing, BCAEN hashing, Tor circuit establishment — happens before the emergency, not during it. The protocol functions like pre-arranged insurance: the caller has already posted their deposit, already configured their offer, already selected their insurer if applicable. When the alert fires, the protocol merely executes what was already arranged. This is actually *less* friction than calling 911, where a panicked caller must verbally convince a skeptical human dispatcher that the threat is real. In Bitaid, the pre-committed capital is the credibility signal; no persuasion is required.

**Q: Won’t the arbitrator pool become an oligopoly?**

A: The concern assumes arbitrators are judging complex moral questions or nuanced liability disputes. They are not. The scope is fundamentally binary: did the responder arrive? Did they stream to BCAEN? Did the outcome condition trigger? Factual deviation is caught at the 40% dissent threshold, which triggers a 100-pool review. An arbitrator who consistently rules unfairly sees their coherence score collapse, their bond forfeited, and their revenue destroyed. Ideological bias is structurally unprofitable because the disputes are too narrow for ideology to hide in. The calibration coherence competition ensures that demand flees from biased courts, and the economic incentive to be factually accurate is absolute because unfair rulings cannibalise the arbitrator’s own income stream.

**Q: How does the network bootstrap when no responders exist in a cell?**

A: All permissionless networks begin vulnerable. Bitcoin survived because the value proposition outpaced the attack surface. Bitaid follows the same pattern: insurers act as venture capitalists, absorbing early risk to capture mature-market returns; drone reality capture provides value even without human responders; alarm systems create demand-side anchors; and a pre-selected launch cohort, tested against the canon, staffs arbitration from day one. The cold start is not a protocol defect — it is an economic opportunity that rewards participants willing to build history before the flywheel compounds.

**Q: Can a wealthy attacker buy their way into control?**

A: The bond is "anti-staking": it earns no yield, grants no governance rights, and carries only downside. A wealthy attacker who buys many seats must still earn labor history (cannot be purchased), survive temporal seasoning (cannot be accelerated), pass calibration coherence (cannot be manufactured), and maintain geographic presence (cell-scoped, cannot be centralised). The asymmetric penalty means that every incoherent vote costs 2×–4× the fee. The 100-pool premium means that confirmed fraud produces capital that funds stronger detection of future fraud. The system is anti-fragile: attacks strengthen the defences they target.

**Q: Why would anyone join as a responder given the legal and liability friction?**

A: Because the opportunity cannot be ignored. Actors reason from opportunity first and find the compliance path afterward. A licensed security guard encountering an incident bounty worth one third of his monthly salary for a two-hour engagement is not consulting a lawyer — he is evaluating whether the risk is worth the reward. The legal scaffolding already exists: citizen’s arrest rights, private security licensing, independent contractor status. It gets used when the economic incentive is sufficient to motivate someone to use it.

Three actor profiles illustrate the pattern. The **specialist responder** — a security professional, ex-military, or licensed guard — recognises that Bitaid lets him monetise existing skills at dramatically higher margin than salaried employment. The legal question was answered when his license was structured; the economic question answers itself. The **self-insuring caller** faces an extreme asymmetry: expected subscription cost is low, expected cost of needing emergency response without coverage is unbounded. No legal navigation required — posting a bounty is legally indistinguishable from hiring a private security firm on retainer. The **vertically integrated insurer** covering a closed community — a condominium association, an industrial estate — can dispatch the same team that resolves the incident, capturing the premium, recovering operational cost through the outcome reward, and reducing the claim liability by intervening before harm compounds. The insurer is solving a profitability problem, not a legal one.

None of these actors requires new law or new regulatory categories. Spanish *des-okupa* firms, American bail bondsmen, South African armed response companies — each operates within legal constraints that appear prohibitive on paper; each exists because economic demand was sufficient to motivate the legal creativity to carve out the space. Bitaid does not require new legal theory. It requires an opportunity large enough that existing practitioners find it worth entering.

Track 3 The Foundations The philosophical and economic underpinnings — Austrian theory, time preference, and the calculation problem applied to emergency services

## 17. Theoretical Foundations

Philosophical backbone — why the mechanism is structurally sound, not merely technically clever

Austrian monetary theory, time preference, the calculation problem applied to emergency services, and full-reserve deposit insurance — not as academic decoration but as the load-bearing structural theory of the design. The Austrian school provides the *why*; the protocol provides the *how*.

**The Austrian School proved why monopoly public safety must fail. Bitaid is what you build when you accept those proofs and stop trying to reform the monopoly.** The following sections rank the theoretical foundations by their load-bearing importance to the protocol architecture. The first three are structural: remove any one and the design collapses. The next five are reinforcing: they make the mechanism more robust, more honest, and harder to capture. The final two are supplementary: they explain why the design choices are recognisable instances of broader principles. None are decorative.

### B.1 The Economic Calculation Problem in Public Safety — Foundation 1 of 10 (Load-bearing)
