**Without prices, rational calculation is impossible. Full stop.** Mises did not say this was difficult or inconvenient. He said it was impossible — a logical category error, not a practical shortcoming. Ludwig von Mises demonstrated in *Socialism* (1922)[23] and *Human Action* (1949)[24] that rational economic calculation is impossible without market prices. Applied to public safety: how many officers should patrol a given block? Bureaucratic allocation cannot answer this — no price mechanism communicates the relevant information. Bitaid resolves this by introducing three price signals that emerge from voluntary interaction: the incident deposit, the bounty, and the outcome reward. Together they constitute a nascent price system for emergency response services — the first in which demand is expressed through voluntary capital commitment rather than bureaucratic allocation.

### B.2 The Knowledge Problem and Decentralised Coordination — Foundation 2 of 10 (Load-bearing)

**The information needed to respond is local, ephemeral, and unavailable to any central authority.** Friedrich A. Hayek’s 1945 essay “The Use of Knowledge in Society”[11] established that information required for economic coordination is dispersed, local, and tacit. A crime in progress is precisely this kind of knowledge: known immediately and with certainty by the victim, becoming stale within minutes. Centralised emergency dispatch introduces a communication bottleneck that destroys the time-value of this information. Bitaid is a decentralised telecommunications protocol for local, ephemeral knowledge — the alert reaches nearest available responders directly, without routing through a dispatch centre. The same principle governs the per-node acceptance threshold mechanism: each operator’s threshold encodes local information about what constitutes meaningful skin-in-the-game, information no central parameter setter could hold.

### B.3 Private Defense and the Market for Security — Foundation 3 of 10 (Load-bearing)

**The monopoly on security is not a feature of civilisation. It is a contingent historical arrangement that has been treated as inevitable because no technology existed to route around it.**

Murray N. Rothbard’s analysis in *For a New Liberty* (1973)[25] describes how private defense arrangements could function through voluntary contractual relationships rather than coercive monopoly. Bitaid is the protocol layer on which such arrangements can emerge.

Hans-Hermann Hoppe’s “The Private Production of Defense” (1998/2003)[26] develops the architecture in more detail: competing insurance companies as the natural providers of security services, with actuarial incentives to genuinely prevent harm rather than benefit from its recurrence. The insurer’s profit is maximised precisely when its subscriber base experiences minimal loss events — the inverse of monopoly public safety provision, where budget growth tracks failure rather than success. Bitaid’s insurance flywheel (§3, §10.5) is a direct on-chain implementation of this architecture: insurers competing on premium pricing and on the bounty and outcome reward levels they fund, with the prevention of incidents reducing the claims they would otherwise pay against life and property policies.

**Hoppe’s thesis made operational.** Hoppe predicted that competing insurers would employ their own security personnel — guards, patrols, monitors — to deliver prevention services to subscribers. Bitaid specifies the coordination mechanism that makes this operational: the insurer employs guards who are bonded responders in the protocol; the same personnel serve both structural security (scheduled patrols) and emergency response (incident dispatch); the same bond, the same reputation, the same evidence network supports both roles. The structural security retainer is deterrence spend, protecting the insurer’s own underwriting position before any incident occurs; when the insurer’s personnel do respond, the bounty and outcome reward flow back from the policyholder’s own escrow to the insurer that employs them, closing the loop precisely as the actuarial logic above predicts — the insurer is paid, through the protocol, to do the thing that avoids paying a claim. The insurer does not hire emergency staff separately from structural security staff. It hires security staff who participate in the protocol, and the protocol routes emergency demand to them based on earned reputation. This is vertical integration of underwriting, employment, and prevention through a single coordination layer — exactly as Hoppe described, now with script-enforced payment and dispute resolution.

Hoppe’s *Democracy: The God That Failed* (2001)[27] identifies two further structural problems that the protocol turns into features. First, democratic public safety is systematically biased: politicians optimise for metrics measurable before the next election, producing over-enforcement of victimless offences and under-response to victim crimes. Bitaid’s incident deposit mechanism corrects this at the demand side — caller capital, not electoral calendar, determines what receives response. The demand side is sovereign. Second, time preference: institutions that reward long horizons and impose real cost on rapid exit produce better outcomes than those that allow instant entry and instant liquidation. The protocol’s temporal seasoning requirement and arbitrator bond withdrawal delay (§14.9) encode this logic in mechanism. You cannot buy a reputation and immediately cash it out. You cannot corrupt a verdict and vanish. Time is on the side of honesty.

**On market concentration and decentralisation.** Decentralisation in Bitaid is measured by permissionless entry and exit, not by equal market share. A dominant insurer or responder firm that achieves scale through superior service is not a protocol failure; it is information revealing market structure. State coercion — licensing barriers, regulatory capture, subsidies to preferred entrants — creates illegitimate monopolies. Market-winning concentration, where any competitor may enter and challenge the incumbent, is legitimate and optimal at that time. The protocol enforces this through open bond posting, gossip-verifiable reserve ratios, and the fork mechanism (§12): no administrative gatekeeper can prevent entry or protect incumbents.

The opt-in formal arbitration path (§14.11) preserves free choice: parties in pre-existing commercial relationships may select their own dispute resolution mechanism, and no single adjudicatory monopoly is assumed. The market determines which mechanism resolves which class of dispute.

**Historical precedent.** The bonded guardian with reputation-forfeiture is not a novel mechanism. Medieval knighthood operated on the same incentive topology: the knight held land and title conditionally on conduct (the fief was forfeit on violation of the protection oath), his reputation was his only real asset in a world before credit scores, and compensation came through patronage and tournament prizes rather than bureaucratic salary. Attainder — strip land, title, heraldry — was the medieval bond seizure. The knight who protected pilgrims earned patronage; the knight who broke his oath lost his standing entirely. Bitaid converges on the same natural equilibrium for decentralised protection markets through different technical means: self-posted collateral instead of aristocratic fief, coherence competition (scored against established consensus) instead of tournament circuit (scored by herald marshals), script-enforced forfeiture instead of royal attainder, and a neutral arbitrator pool instead of the court of chivalry. The mechanism is robust across eight centuries of radically different social substrates.

**The technology asymmetry.** The centralization of the past five centuries is better understood as a technological artefact than as a political necessity. Gunpowder destroyed feudal fortifications and enabled standing armies that no local levy could match. The printing press scaled propaganda before it scaled dissent — a single pamphleteer could reach thousands, but only the state could reach millions through controlled distribution. Central banking created fiscal capacity — debt-funded war and welfare — that no local authority could replicate. Broadcast media concentrated narrative control in whoever owned the transmitter. In each case, the advantage accrued to the actor with the largest apparatus; the bigger bully snowballed because coercion and information control scaled faster than defense.[40]

The current wave inverts this pattern. Encryption scales with key length, not headcount — a single pseudonymous keypair resists a nation-state’s surveillance apparatus more effectively than an institution with a thousand employees. A conditional Bitcoin escrow enforces payment without a court system, a banking relationship, or a tax authority. A Tor Hidden Service routes around censorship regardless of how many regulators the censor employs. Content-addressed storage with cryptographic anchoring produces evidence that cannot be suppressed without compromising every archival node simultaneously. The defensive advantage has returned.

The pattern of history is not that centralized rule is natural. It is that technology determined whether offense or defense had the advantage. Feudalism was decentralized because the defensive advantage of a stone wall exceeded the offensive advantage of a siege ladder. The modern state centralized because the telephone, the tank, and the printing press gave the attacker overwhelming advantage. Bitaid is possible because cryptography and distributed consensus make pseudonymous, trust-minimised coordination stronger than the institutions that would capture or suppress it. The protocol does not require the state to collapse. It requires only that the defensive advantage persists long enough for the market to discover it.

**The NAP inside, legal risk outside.** Within the protocol the non-aggression principle is the rule. The checklists of §14.12 reduce every dispute to simple observable questions — who initiated force, whether a threat was really suppressed, whether property was taken back from the person who took it — and the canon annotates its cases by those questions, so verdicts, and with them every payout and forfeiture, are engineered to converge on the NAP (§14.3, §14.12). What the protocol cannot do is impose the NAP on the world around it. Local law stays whatever it is, and the gap between the two is priced. A responder operating in a jurisdiction where citizen arrest is narrow and liability exposure is high will demand a higher bounty and a larger outcome reward to compensate. A caller posting a low bounty in that jurisdiction receives no response. The market clears where the price meets the local legal reality. The protocol does not harmonise the world’s legal systems; it judges conduct by one principle inside, asks each participant to bear their own jurisdictional risk outside, and reveals the cost of that risk through the three-tier offer. Legal variance becomes price variance. Price variance becomes information. The market aggregates what no central authority could compute.

### B.4 Entrepreneurial Alertness and the Responder Function — Foundation 4 of 10

The market discovers who can best serve a need. No bureaucracy can do this in advance.

Israel M. Kirzner’s *Competition and Entrepreneurship* (1973)[14] defines the entrepreneur as an agent alert to unexploited profit opportunities. The responder in the Bitaid network functions as a Kirznerian entrepreneur: they observe a local alert with a bounty, assess their proximity and capability, and act to close the gap between unserved demand for safety and available supply of response capacity. Jesús Huerta de Soto’s work on dynamic efficiency[39] extends this to tacit knowledge — the kind that cannot be centralised or priced by a bureaucratic system. A veteran who knows how to de-escalate a knife confrontation without force, a former paramedic who carries a trauma kit, a trained martial artist who can restrain an aggressor proportionately — these are capabilities the formal system cannot route demand to because it has no price mechanism for doing so. Bitaid transforms this distributed, tacit capability into productive action through the bounty mechanism: the market routes demand to whoever can best serve it, not to whoever the dispatch algorithm assigns.

The three-tier offer is the discovery procedure itself. The incident deposit is the caller’s revealed preference for seriousness — no mechanism other than voluntary capital commitment communicates urgency with equal precision. The bounty is the market-clearing wage for presence: the price at which willing labor meets expressed demand. The outcome reward is the Schumpeterian profit for solving the problem — the return to entrepreneurial action that identified and closed the gap. These three numbers, broadcast simultaneously to every responder in the cell, aggregate local information about the true cost of safety that no central planner could hold. The responder who accepts reveals that they can serve profitably at that price. The responder who passes reveals that they cannot. The pattern of accepted and rejected offers over time constitutes a price discovery process for emergency response at the resolution of individual incidents — something no dispatch algorithm has ever produced.

### B.5 The Juridical Distinction Between Deposit and Insurance — Foundation 5 of 10

Sound insurance is not fractional-reserve banking. The juridical distinction is the whole argument.

Jesús Huerta de Soto’s *Money, Bank Credit, and Economic Cycles* (1998/2006)[28] develops the juridical foundations distinguishing deposit contracts (irregular deposits requiring 100% availability) from loan contracts and from insurance contracts (where actuarial pooling against contingent events is the contract’s defining feature, not a violation of it). His critique of fractional-reserve banking targets the specific case where a deposit contract is performed as a loan contract — a juridical category error that manufactures phantom money substitutes. Insurance contracts fall outside this critique entirely.

Bitaid’s bond architecture (§10.1, §10.3) enforces full reservation at the script level. Every bond, whether personal or insurer-backed, is a UTXO locked in a conditional escrow with deterministic spending paths. There is no fractional reserve.

A personal bond and an insurer buffer are identical in form. Both are UTXOs. Both are seized on forfeiture. Both are fully reserved on-chain. The difference is in deployment pattern, not deposit structure. A personal bond backs one identity with one-for-one capital. An insurer buffer backs many identities by posting many UTXOs into many escrows simultaneously — actuarially confident because emergencies are sequential, not simultaneous, for any single backed identity.

The attestation matching the verdict (§14.11), combined with the pre-committed adaptor signature for that outcome, materialises the result. It selects which spending path activates per incident, consuming the buffer where misuse occurred while leaving other encumbrances intact. The script handles the selection natively; the insurer does not rely on an accounting layer to track exposure.

The zero protocol weight applies to scoring only. The buffer is stripped before computing arbitrator selection, merit weight, or labor qualification. Relay operators and responder nodes may subjectively factor insurer backing into their own acceptance thresholds — treating a substantial buffer as a signal that someone with capital already vetted this participant. This is opt-in and operator-specific, not protocol-mandated. The market may price the risk differential; the protocol does not.

The actuarial multiplication happens where it belongs: at the insurer’s balance sheet, not at the deposit contract. The protocol enforces identical cryptographic treatment on both; the juridical tradition’s deposit-insurance distinction is preserved not by different script structures but by the same script structure applied to different risk deployments.

### B.6 The Monetary Foundation of the Stake — Foundation 6 of 10

Sound collateral requires sound, trustless, and auditable money. No other asset qualifies.

Gold is sound money by the standard of costly production and fixed supply. But gold fails the trustlessness test: physical custody requires vaults, guards, auditors, and custodians — a chain of trusted intermediaries that the protocol cannot afford. Carl Menger’s analysis of the origin of money (1871,[30] 1892[31]) established that money emerges spontaneously as the most saleable good. Saifedean Ammous, applying this tradition directly to Bitcoin in *The Bitcoin Standard* (2018)[33], states the principle plainly: “For something to assume a monetary role, it has to be costly to produce, otherwise the temptation to make money on the cheap will destroy the wealth of the savers.” Bitcoin’s proof-of-work mechanism makes its production provably costly and its supply schedule provably fixed.

For Bitaid’s bond mechanism, sound money is necessary but not sufficient. Four additional properties are required. **Programmability:** the bond lives in a conditional Bitcoin script with multiple deterministic spending paths — cooperative release, verdict release, timelocked refund. No custodian is needed to enforce the conditions; the script enforces them natively. Gold cannot be programmed; fiat requires banking infrastructure that introduces trusted intermediaries. **Auditability:** any node can verify that a bond UTXO exists on-chain, that it is unspent, and that its script conditions match the protocol specification. No auditor, no vault inspection, no counterparty trust. **Settlement finality:** once a bond transaction is confirmed, it cannot be reversed by any authority. The deterrent value of a bond that can be frozen by a court order or seized by a banking regulator is a deterrent in name only. **Permissionless access:** anyone with internet access can acquire Bitcoin and post a bond. No bank account, no identification, no jurisdiction, no minimum wealth threshold beyond what the protocol itself sets. Gold requires physical possession; fiat requires institutional access. Bitcoin requires only a keypair and a network connection.

A bond denominated in an inflatable currency is a bond whose deterrent value can be silently eroded by whoever controls that currency’s supply. A bond denominated in Bitcoin cannot be debased by any institution, any government, or any protocol change. But the deeper point is that a bond denominated in a non-programmable asset is a bond that cannot enforce its own conditions. The protocol requires not just sound money but *scriptable* sound money — money whose rules execute without human intervention. Only Bitcoin combines both.

Eugen von Böhm-Bawerk’s theory of capital and interest (1884)[32] frames the bond in terms of time preference. The caller who locks capital in escrow demonstrates a low time preference: willingness to forgo immediate use of that capital to secure the future benefit of a credible commitment. Responders who invest time building reputation through the rating system demonstrate the same. The protocol rewards low time preference and filters out high-time-preference actors.

The temporal seasoning function encodes Böhm-Bawerk’s roundabout production principle directly in protocol parameters. `min(1, days/90)` is not merely an anti-Sybil heuristic — it is a formal statement that real capital formation requires irreplaceable calendar time and cannot be accelerated by spending more money. A Sybil farm completing 90 incidents in one week receives a labor score multiplied by approximately 0.078. A responder completing 90 incidents over 90 days receives full weight. The protocol does not merely count effort; it weights effort by time that cannot be manufactured or purchased. A malicious actor cannot buy seasoned labor any more than a central bank can build a factory by expanding the money supply. The Sybil farm and the central bank face the same wall: the roundabout cannot be shortened by spending harder.

### B.7 Robust Institutions and Spontaneous Order — Foundation 7 of 10

The best institutions do not require good actors. They produce good outcomes regardless.

Peter Boettke’s work on robust political economy[34] analyses institutions that function well not because they assume benevolent actors, but because they align individual incentives with socially beneficial outcomes regardless of actor motivation. Bitaid is a robust institution: it does not require responders to be altruistic, callers to be honest, or arbitrators to be wise. F.A. Hayek’s concept of spontaneous order (*Law, Legislation and Liberty*, 1973)[12] describes the emergence of complex, beneficial social arrangements from local actions of individuals following simple rules, without central design. Bitaid’s network is a spontaneous order: no authority decides which responders attend which incidents. These outcomes emerge from voluntary interactions of staked participants following the protocol’s incentive rules. The order is not designed. It arises.

### B.8 The Roundabout Continuation — Foundation 8 of 10

**If money can be taken from the state, so can the rest.** The strategy is not reform. It is replacement by superior alternative.

In a 1984 interview with the Cato Institute,[36] Friedrich Hayek offered what has become the most cited remark in the Bitcoin tradition:

> “I don’t believe we shall ever have a good money again before we take the thing out of the hands of government. That is, we can’t take them violently out of the hands of government. All we can do is by some sly roundabout way introduce something that they can’t stop.”

The intuition is strategic, not theoretical: state monopolies are not undone by argument or by direct contest; they are undone by quietly building an alternative that is sufficiently better and sufficiently distributed that suppression becomes either technically impossible or politically too costly to attempt. Hayek had imagined this for money, anticipating denationalised currency in *Denationalisation of Money* (1976)[35] while doubting the political path remained open. The cypherpunk movement — Tim May’s *Crypto Anarchist Manifesto* (1988)[37], Eric Hughes’s *A Cypherpunk’s Manifesto* (1993)[38] with its operative thesis “cypherpunks write code” — formalised the missing tactic: cryptography as the substrate on which the roundabout could actually be built. Bitcoin made the strategy real.

What was true of money is structurally true of every domain where the state operates as a monopoly provider. Money is one such monopoly; security is another; adjudication a third; archival evidence a fourth; identity a fifth. The Hayekian strategy generalises: where a state monopoly delivers a service the market would deliver better, the path forward is neither armed contest nor political persuasion but the construction of a permissionless protocol that performs the service so credibly that ordinary people prefer it. The state retains nominal jurisdiction; the actual coordination migrates elsewhere. The monopoly is not abolished — it is rendered marginal.

Bitaid is the continuation of this strategy applied to public safety. The protocol does not lobby for police reform, propose a constitutional amendment, or contest the state’s claim to a monopoly on the legitimate use of force. It builds an alternative coordination layer for emergency response — denominated in money the state cannot debase, settled through escrows the state cannot freeze, witnessed by evidence the state cannot suppress, and adjudicated by mechanisms the state cannot capture. If it works, it works regardless of whether any state recognises it. If it does not work, no state had to be persuaded of anything. The roundabout is the whole strategy.

This is the cypherpunk thesis at full extension: the role of technology in a world of state monopolies is not to argue against the monopolies but to render them irrelevant by competing with them at the protocol layer. Bitcoin removed the monetary monopoly from the conversation. Bitaid attempts the same removal for the public-safety monopoly. The pattern is general; the targets are many; the method is the same.

### B.9 Trust Minimisation as Design Principle — Foundation 9 of 10 (Supplementary)

Nick Szabo’s concept of trust minimisation[22] is the design principle underlying every escrow script, every blind vote, every hash-before-upload ordering. Szabo’s central observation is that “trustless” systems do not exist; rather, systems can be *trust-minimised* — engineered to require the least possible mutual trust between parties to operate correctly. The protocol does not eliminate trust; it makes trust unnecessary in the specific places where trust has historically been exploited.

**Governance minimisation.** Szabo argues that the hardest and most important part of trust minimisation is governance minimisation — reducing the surface area where human discretion can alter rules. Bitaid carries this to its logical conclusion: there is no governance token, no DAO, no admin key, no multi-sig committee with upgrade authority, and no on-chain voting mechanism that could capture the protocol. The rules are baked into Bitcoin script and game-theoretic incentive alignment. The arbitrator bond floor is fixed at 0.1 BTC, pool entry is via coherence competition rather than governance vote, and no mechanism exists that a well-capitalised attacker could capture by majority vote. The only “upgrade” path is the fork mechanism (§12): any node may run modified software, but nobody can force adoption on others. Custody minimisation follows the same logic at the execution layer. Verdict release is not purely cryptographic: a bonded oracle (§15.1, §15.5) decides which pre-committed route an escrow takes, so it holds effective custody of that choice. What it cannot do is create a route. Every output a verdict can produce is fixed in the script before the dispute, so a coerced, bribed or captured oracle can at worst select the wrong pre-built outcome, never send funds outside the contract. The oracle is credible the same way every other role is — bonded, labor-proven and coherence-tested (PoB+L+C) — not because anyone trusts it.

**Lean design over feature creep.** Szabo cautions against adding features that compromise security and decentralisation for marginal functionality. Bitaid is a coordination protocol, not a platform. The three-tier offer (deposit + bounty + outcome reward) is minimal but sufficient to price every behaviour the protocol needs to incentivise. The BCAEN stores evidence, not arbitrary data — this is a deliberate legal-attack-surface reduction. Szabo warns that no decentralised network operates in a legal vacuum, and storing arbitrary data on-chain can unintentionally worsen regulatory exposure. BCAEN’s content-addressed, permissionless-upload design ensures the network cannot be characterised as a “publisher” or “platform” under existing legal frameworks; it is a storage market with cryptographic integrity guarantees.

**Bitcoin as trust-minimised insurance.** Szabo views Bitcoin as an optimal form of insurance against systemic financial failure — replacing reliance on banks or governments with mathematical guarantees. Bitaid extends this logic to physical safety: the bond is trust-minimised insurance against bad behaviour. The caller’s deposit insures against false alerts; the responder’s bond insures against misconduct; the arbitrator bond insures against corrupt verdicts. All are denominated in Bitcoin precisely because Bitcoin’s supply schedule, settlement finality, and censorship resistance make it the only asset that cannot be silently debased or politically frozen — properties that fiat-denominated bonds, held in regulated custody, fundamentally lack. The bond is not merely collateral; it is a Szabo-style insurance contract where the insured event (misconduct) triggers an automatic, non-discretionary payout (forfeiture) enforced by script rather than by any human authority.

The arbitrator pool replaces trust in judicial institutions with game-theoretic incentive alignment. The escrow scripts replace trust in payment intermediaries with cryptographic enforcement. The BCAEN replaces trust in evidence custodians with content addressing and demand-driven availability enforcement. Each layer asks: where has trust historically been exploited, and what mechanism removes the need for it? That question, iterated across every component, is the design method.

### B.10 The Productivity of Violence — Foundation 10 of 10 (Supplementary)

Mancur Olson’s distinction between roving and stationary bandits[29] explains why the protocol’s insurance flywheel works: stationary protectors (insurers with ongoing subscriber relationships) have incentives to prevent incidents, while roving predators (one-time attackers) cannot sustain operations against a bonded, pseudonymous, globally distributed network. The flywheel converts the stationary bandit dynamic from a threat to a feature: insurers profit from prevention, not from post-incident payment.

**The monopoly on force is not abolished — it is subjected, for the first time, to genuine market competition.** Bitaid provides the coordination layer that aggregates dispersed demand for private safety services into legible price signals. The Austrian school supplied the theoretical proof that this was necessary: monopoly provision cannot calculate, cannot discover local knowledge, and cannot align insurer incentives with prevention. The protocol supplies the mechanism: three-tier price signals, script-enforced escrows, coherence-gated arbitration, and full-reserve insurance that cannot be debased or captured. Where the network reaches sufficient density, the state monopoly faces a competitor that calculates better, responds faster, and pays its participants for outcomes rather than inputs. The monopoly need not collapse. It need only become the second choice.

## 18. Appendix A — Protocol Parameter Reference

All protocol parameters at genesis

Parameters marked *deflating* follow the 20% annual nominal schedule computed from Bitaid’s application-layer genesis block height. Parameters marked *calibrated* are set at genesis and adjustable only through the governance-free fork mechanism (§12). Block counts for protocol windows are Bitcoin block heights (~10 minutes per block), the clock of the anchored event log (§12); script timelocks are stated in wall-clock time with a block count per chain (§15.1).

**Which parameters bear the load.** Not every number below matters equally. The security argument depends on a few: the ⅔ labor-qualified invariant, the stake-above-exposure gate, 90-day seasoning, the roughly 97% break-even set by the penalty and momentum rules, the review sampling rate, and the 80% eligibility floor. Changing one of these changes what an attack costs, and each should move only with simulation behind it. Others are expected to be found by the market and can be tuned freely: fee levels, outcome bucket sizes, offer sizes, the intervener/documenter split, and insurer layer pricing. The rest are operational windows and floors. A machine-readable copy of this table (the parameter file) accompanies the paper, so that simulations and the reference implementation load the same values.

### Bonds & Floors

Entry cost tracks the liability each role assumes. A caller risks their deposit against a misuse ruling. A responder risks their bond against misuse and bad-faith arbitration, with their physical conduct on camera and subject to arbitration review. An arbitrator risks 2× to 4× the per-case fee, deducted from their bond, on each incoherent vote. An archival node risks proportional forfeiture against failure to serve authenticated retrieval requests. The ratios are deliberate: caller at 5,000 sat, responder at 0.01 BTC (200×), arbitrator at 0.1 BTC (2,000×), archival at 0.005 BTC (100×). The caller floor deflates at 20% annually to preserve accessibility regardless of Bitcoin’s price trajectory; service-provider floors carry no deflation schedule, so Bitcoin appreciation passively filters toward professionalisation on the supply side.

### Arbitrator pool Entry

The pool is closed by default with a target size equal to the rolling median of active arbitrators over 200 case-event windows. Slots open only on natural turnover (exit, ejection, forfeiture) or sustained demand signal (72 hours of non-zero queue after price-out ejection). Entry operates on three tracks depending on calibration library maturity. Track A (coherence competition, library ≥ 100 cases) requires applicants to answer 15 cases — 10 live shadow votes and 5 canon cases — pay a non-refundable BCAEN participation fee, and lock a scaled intention bond. Track B (invitation) lets proven arbitrators vouch for candidates. Track C (weighted lottery) is a fallback for a launch whose canon holds fewer than 100 cases; under the planned launch it is dormant. The 100-case threshold for Track A activation balances the need for a meaningful calibration library against the risk of prolonged lottery-based entry during genesis.

### Arbitration mechanism

The ⅔ labor-qualified invariant is the core Sybil defence at the panel level: it guarantees that no panel can be majority-Sybil regardless of how many fake identities enter the pool. The 80% coherence threshold is the eligibility floor; with recurrence-weighted penalties, serving pays only above about 97% coherence. Configurable quality settings (coherence threshold, open seat fraction, bond minimum) create a tiered premium service market for adjudication quality. The bond minimum decays at ×0.70 per 144-block interval to prevent deadlock when high settings cannot fill a panel.

### Fee Market

The dual-trigger mechanism adapts EIP-1559 with a time-based congestion trigger that fires even when no cases are closing. The asymmetry is deliberate: fees rise from both case closes and time during congestion, but fall only on successful case clears. By 72 hours of continuous congestion the time trigger alone multiplies the fee by about 4.3× (1.0625^24); each case close while the queue is non-zero adds a further 12.5%, so with ten closes in that window the fee is about 14× its starting level. There is no protocol-imposed floor; the market clears wherever supply and demand meet. The 36-block price-out grace window ensures queue depth signals are clean: every case in the queue at block H is fundable at fee(H).

### Forfeiture Flows

All forfeiture events route through a consistent framework: panel fees first at normal case rate, then type-specific remainder destinations. No forfeiture event enriches the ruling panel beyond their standard per-case fee — this closes the captured-panel attack where a majority might falsely condemn honest participants to extract seized bonds.

### Network & Routing

Dead-end scoring enforces liveness without a relay bond: nodes that fail to propagate see reduced routing priority and eventual exclusion. The thresholds (0.3 for deprioritisation, 0.6 for exclusion) use rolling windows and count only failures after a signed receipt, so a network split is not scored and an excluded node can recover through probation. Grid cells at H3 resolution 7 (~5 km², ~1.4 km edge) balance geographic precision against propagation overhead.

### Evidence (BCAEN)

Chunk size at 256 KB provides standard-definition footage intervals. The 90-day BCAEN subsidy drip period releases retroactive forfeiture gradually to avoid market shock while attracting new archival nodes through temporarily elevated yields.

## 19. Appendix B — Implementation Reference (Draft)

First draft for implementers and reviewers — outputs, transactions, messages, the oracle's nonce protocol, availability checks and provisional parameters

This appendix turns the specification into something code can be written against. It is a first draft: every construction here needs per-chain test vectors, benchmarks and, for B.3, review by a cryptographer before funds depend on it (§15.5). Where the main text and this appendix differ, the main text states the intent and this appendix is the proposal for meeting it.

### B.1 Outputs and transactions

All outputs are Taproot (BIP341) on both chains. Every signature is BIP340 Schnorr with `SIGHASH_ALL` (the default sighash). On Bitcoin L1, every pre-signed transaction carries an anchor output so that whoever broadcasts it can raise its fee by child-pays-for-parent; on Liquid, fees are explicit outputs set at signing.

| Output | Key path | Script leaves | Spent by |
|---|---|---|---|
| Funding (caller) | Unspendable (NUMS point) | `<W> OP_CHECKSIGVERIFY <P_oracle> OP_CHECKSIG`; `<K_scoped> OP_CHECKSIG`; `<T_fallback> OP_CHECKLOCKTIMEVERIFY OP_DROP <W> OP_CHECKSIG` | Withdrawal co-signed by the oracle when no alert or case is open; scoped key to sign the incident transaction; wallet alone after the fallback |
| Incident | MuSig2(K_scoped, R₁ … Rₙ), no script tree | none | Cooperative close; a pre-signed payout completed by the oracle's attestation; the pre-signed refund |
| Bond (responder, arbitrator, archivist, delegate) | Unspendable (NUMS point) | `<K_op> OP_CHECKSIGVERIFY <P_oracle> OP_CHECKSIG`; `<T_fallback> OP_CHECKLOCKTIMEVERIFY OP_DROP <W_op> OP_CHECKSIG` | Withdrawal after the hold or a pre-signed forfeiture, each co-signed by the oracle; the operator's wallet alone after the fallback time |
| Insurer buffer | Insurer's wallet key | `<K_ins_scoped> OP_CHECKSIG` | As the funding output, signed by the insurer's client at acceptance |

*Incident transaction.* Inputs: the caller's funding outputs and any insurer buffer outputs, in the creation order of §10.5. Outputs: one incident output holding the total, plus an anchor on Bitcoin L1. Signed at acceptance by the scoped keys and broadcast at once.

*Payouts.* One transaction per outcome, each spending the incident output by key path. All signers produce MuSig2 partial adaptor signatures, each encrypted to the attestation point S_m of that outcome (B.3). When the oracle publishes its attestation s for outcome m, anyone adds s to complete the signature for that payout and broadcasts it. Recipients are fresh per-case addresses for responders (§16.2), the caller's committed refund address, and, for forfeitures, the addresses fixed by §14.8.

*Refund.* One transaction spending the incident output by key path, with `nLockTime` set 90 days after acceptance, returning each party's contribution. Fully signed at acceptance; valid only after the locktime.

*Cooperative close.* Signed by all incident signers when the attending vote settles without escalation; spends the incident output by key path.

*Bond forfeitures.* Pre-signed by the operator's scoped key at the moment of exposure — acceptance for a responder, taking a seat for an arbitrator, slot registration for an archivist's availability deposit, delegation for a signing delegate — paying the destinations §14.8 fixes for that case. The oracle adds its co-signature only after the outcome that triggers that forfeiture is final. Withdrawals need the oracle's co-signature, given once the hold of §14.9 has passed with no review pending.

*Per-chain differences.* Elements signature hashes commit to asset and value commitments, so every template is built and signed per chain. On Liquid, outputs are confidential; parties share blinding factors in the signature package so each can verify amounts, and the oracle receives them for labor and template checks.

### B.2 Messages

Messages are CBOR in canonical encoding, signed with the sender's identity key (BIP340), and carried over Tor for alerts and gossip and over I2P or equivalent for BCAEN. Packages addressed to specific parties are encrypted to them (ECDH and an authenticated cipher). Every message carries a version, a type, the Bitcoin block height at sending, and the sender's identity public key.

| Message | Main fields | Sent by / when |
|---|---|---|
| Registration | funding outpoints, scoped key K, template hash T, wallet signature over (K, T, outpoints), chosen signers (phone, Core, delegates) | Caller, at registration; anchored |
| Alert | case_id, H3 cell, economics (deposit, bounty, reward, split rule), funding outpoints with leaf proof, pre-trigger hash | Caller app, at panic |
| Accept | case_id, responder identity, MuSig2 public key, bond outpoint, payout derivation commitment | Responder app, at acceptance |
| Nonce assignment | case_id, batch, index j, R_j | Deterministic from the log; anchored |
| Signature package | case_id, set version, MuSig2 public nonces, partial signatures for the incident transaction and refund, partial adaptor signatures per outcome | Every signer, during the joining window; encrypted, relayed via peers and BCAEN |
| Tier commitment | case_id, tier, roster root, hash of predicate answers and salt | Panel, after each tier; 32-byte hash in one OP_RETURN |
| Attestation | case_id, index j, outcome id m, s | Oracle, once per case after the last re-roll |
| Availability challenge | chunk hash, node, slot, challenge bond outpoint | Any retriever, on a failed retrieval |

*Outcome identifiers.* Each outcome is a pair (class, split), where the class comes from the dispute-type checklists of §14.12 and the split is the bucketed share vector. Its message is m = tagged_hash("Bitaid/outcome", case_id ‖ set version ‖ class ‖ split).

### B.3 The oracle key and per-case nonces

*Membership.* Arbitrators who have completed probation and seasoning. Threshold t = ⌊n/2⌋ + 1 of the n members at each epoch. An epoch is 2,016 Bitcoin blocks (about two weeks, provisional); membership changes take effect at epoch boundaries.

*Key.* Generated once by distributed key generation (the FROST key generation procedure), then proactively reshared at every epoch boundary so that departing members' shares become useless while the public key P stays the same (D-FROST is the starting point).

*Nonces.* At each epoch the members jointly generate a batch of one-time secrets k_j, each Shamir-shared like the key, and publish the points R_j = k_j·G, negating k_j where needed so that R_j has an even y-coordinate as BIP340 requires. Batch size is set well above the busiest epoch observed (provisionally 10× peak), because a spare nonce costs each member only one stored share. When half the batch has been assigned, the members generate the next batch at once rather than waiting for the epoch boundary. A case accepted while no nonce is free waits in the queue until the next batch arrives — minutes in normal operation — and never proceeds without one; cases already open are unaffected. Unused nonces of open cases are reshared with the key at the epoch boundary; nonces never assigned are discarded.

*Assignment.* When a case is accepted it takes the next unused index j, recorded in the anchored log. For each outcome m, anyone can compute the attestation point S_m = R_j + e_m·P, where e_m is the BIP340 challenge hash of (R_j, P, m). These are the points the payouts' adaptor signatures are encrypted to.

*Attestation.* After the last re-roll window closes, each member replays the anchored log, checks the outcome and that the case's payout set matches the caller's committed template, and releases its partial s_i = k_{j,i} + e_m·λ_i·x_i. Any t valid partials, each checked against the member's public shares, combine into s, and (R_j, s) is a BIP340 signature on m under P. **A member never releases a partial for index j on a second message**; an index is spent once its attestation is posted. Signing a second message with the same nonce would reveal the key.

*Liveness.* Partials are collected asynchronously in the manner of ROAST, so absent members do not stall a case once t have responded. If t cannot be reached, the case waits; the incident refund is the backstop.

*What a captured majority can do.* With t shares of the key and of the nonces, a coalition can attest any outcome for any case: it can push wrong verdicts among the pre-signed payouts, and co-sign early bond withdrawals for operators who also sign. It cannot create a payout no party signed. The oracle's power is therefore *bounded settlement authority*: it chooses which pre-signed outcome becomes executable, never what the outcomes are. A wrong attestation is visible — anyone replaying the anchored log can see that the attested outcome does not follow from the recorded votes — but a threshold signature does not reveal which members signed, so there is no individual slashing for it. The remedy is pool-wide: a fork and a fresh key. The same holds for withdrawal approvals, where a captured majority could delay honest withdrawals up to the 365-day fallback. That is the condition whose cost §14.4 and §14.10 make the protocol's central defence.

### B.4 Scoped keys and signing delegates

*Authorisation.* At registration the caller's wallet signs a message binding the scoped key K to the funding outpoints and to the template hash T. T commits to every caller-side output — the refund address and anything else owed to the caller — and to the rule that the incident transaction's only non-anchor output is the incident output for the responders recorded in the log.

*Holders.* The caller chooses up to three holders: the phone, the caller's Core, or bonded signing delegates. Any one of them can sign; the first valid signature package wins.

*Delegates.* A delegate is a bonded BCAEN node whose bond exceeds the total value of the funding outputs delegated to it (§10.4). At delegation it pre-signs a forfeiture of that bond to the caller. Any spend of a funding output through the scoped-key leaf that is not the template's incident transaction is deterministic evidence; the oracle then co-signs the delegate's forfeiture. Delegates earn a fee per incident signed.

### B.5 Availability verification

1. A retriever whose authenticated retrieval fails posts an **availability challenge** with a small challenge bond.
2. Five arbitrators are drawn by a block hash mined after the challenge. Within 6 blocks each sends ordinary retrieval requests for the chunk over I2P, indistinguishable from any other retrieval, and records the result by commit-reveal.
3. If at least four of the five fail to retrieve the chunk, the node's base-slot availability deposit is forfeited by the pre-signed forfeiture (§14.8), the challenge bond is returned, and the probers are paid from the deposit. Otherwise the challenge fails, the challenge bond pays the probers, and the node is unaffected.

Because the content is addressed by hash, a single successful retrieval proves availability at that moment. Because probes look like ordinary retrievals, a node cannot serve only the probers. Three rules keep the check from becoming a griefing tool: the five probers are drawn from different regions, so a local outage or network partition cannot make an honest node look absent; a node that fails a check gets a recovery window of 6 blocks to serve the chunk before forfeiture; and repeated challenges against the same node within 144 blocks require doubling challenge bonds.

### B.6 Provisional parameter choices

| Decision | Choice | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| Review sampling rate R | 5% up to 0.01 BTC; 10% up to 0.1 BTC; 20% up to 1 BTC; 40% above | Detection must beat gain / stake; larger cases justify more review |
| Track A exam | 15 cases: 10 live shadow votes, 5 canon | Equal per-case weight then gives ⅔ live, ⅓ canon |
| 3-arbitrator penalty floor | 2× the fee (unchanged) | On a 3-panel a lone dissenter may be the honest one; re-rolls, not a harsher floor, resolve that case |
| Release and joining window | 120 seconds | Long enough for auto-accept and signing over Tor; short enough not to delay dispatch |
| Signing responders per incident set | At most 5 | Bounds the payout set; more responders may attend as documenters outside the escrow or join by a new incident transaction |
| Split buckets | 10% steps for up to 3 signing responders; 20% steps for 4–5 | At most 126 splits per outcome class (5 responders, 20% steps); a few hundred to under a thousand payouts per incident across classes, to be benchmarked on phones |
| Incident refund locktime | 90 days | Longer than any ordinary dispute including re-rolls |
| Bond fallback timelock | 365 days, renewed by re-posting | Bonds are never trapped if the oracle disappears |
| Oracle epoch | 2,016 Bitcoin blocks | Resharing cadence and membership updates |
| Oracle nonce reserve | 10× the busiest epoch observed; next batch generated when half is assigned | A surge never waits on nonces; a spare costs each member one stored share |
| Availability check | 5 probers, 4 must fail, 6-block window | Single honest successful retrieval clears the node |
| Dissent momentum | λ = 0.99 per case, 2^D capped at 32×, review at D ≥ 3 | Break-even about 97% coherence; at 99% coherence income stays within 5% of a flat penalty |
| Challenge bond | 3× the case fee; vindication returns it with the penalty refunded, the case fee and half the wrong majority's penalties | Lets an honest dissenter escape a wrong penalty, which is what makes a 97% bar fair |

### B.7 Genesis checklist (provisional)

| Item | Provisional value | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Starting arbitration fee | 0.0002 BTC per arbitrator seat | Fee market (§14.6) moves it from there |
| Initial arbitrator pool | About 50 pre-selected; network opens once at least 31 have qualified | Launch-cohort members qualify by scoring at least 97% per predicate on 10 canon cases; this route closes once 31 have qualified through real cases (§14.3) |
| Review panel before the pool reaches 100 | The whole qualified pool, minus the case's own arbitrators | However small; becomes the 100-arbitrator review once the pool allows |
| Canon at launch | At least 100 annotated cases, real and written | Track A opens once the library holds 100 cases (§14.3) |
| Oracle key | Generated by the launch pool through distributed key generation | Reshared each epoch as membership changes (B.3) |
| Bootstrap nodes | A published list, replaceable in configuration | No node on the list has protocol authority |

### B.8 Minimum viable path

A first implementation does not need the whole protocol. The smallest loop that runs real incidents end to end is:

- **Registration:** the funding output, the scoped key and the committed caller template (§10.3, B.4).
- **Alert:** the geofenced alert with its economic offer (§12).
- **Acceptance:** the incident transaction and the pre-signed refund, with a single payout per responder share and no oracle yet (§10.3).
- **Evidence:** hash-before-upload to a small set of archival nodes (§13).
- **Settlement:** the attending vote and cooperative close (§14.1).

That loop already delivers the core promise: a bonded responder answers a bonded alert, the evidence is pinned, and payment settles by agreement or by refund. Everything else is added in layers, each testable on its own: the oracle and pre-signed outcome payouts (B.1, B.3); arbitration, re-rolls and the challenge bond (§14.4–§14.5); review sampling and momentum (§14.3–§14.4); insurer buffer stacks (§10.5); Track B entry. Building in this order means the first incidents test the parts every later layer depends on.
